Littlewood-Paley square functions and the local Hardy space for Multi-Norm Structures on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} Thanks: 1 Instytut Matematyczny, Uniwersytet Wrocławski, Pl. Grunwaldzki 2, 50-384 Wrocław, xxxxx agnieszka.hejna@math.uni.wroc.pl Thanks: 2 University of Wisconsin-Madison, Madison, WI 53706, ajnagel@ me.com Thanks: 3 Scuola Normale Superiore, Piazza dei Cavalieri 7, 56126 Pisa, fulvio.ricci@sns.it
Agnieszka Hejna 1, Alexander Nagel 2, Fulvio Ricci 3

Abstract
. Multi-norm singular integrals and Fourier multipliers were introduced in [ 29 ] , and one application of these notions was a precise description of the composition of convolution operators with Calderón-Zygmund kernels adapted to n n different families of dilations. The description of the resulting operators was given in terms of differential inequalities specified by a matrix 𝐄 \mathbf{E} , and in terms of dyadic decompositions of the kernels and multipliers. In this paper we extend the analysis of multi-norm structures on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} by studying the induced Littlewood-Paley decomposition of the frequency space and various associated square functions. After establishing their L 1 L^{1} -equivalence, we use these square functions to define a local multi-norm Hardy space 𝐡 𝐄 1 ​ ( ℝ d ) \mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . We give several equivalent descriptions of this space, including an atomic characterization. There has been recent work, limited to the 2 2 -dilation case, by other authors. The general n n -dilation case treated here is considerably harder and requires new ideas and a more systematic approach.



1.
Introduction

This paper studies Littlewood-Paley theory, square functions, and the associated local Hardy space in the context of the multi-norm theory formally introduced, though not with this name, in
[
29
]
.
The main objective of
[
29
]
was to identify a class of singular integral operators that includes the composition of several given one-parameter Calderón-Zygmund operators having different homogeneities. This class is characterized by differential inequalities on the multipliers or equivalent differential inequalities and cancellation conditions on the kernels. These kernels and multipliers also have dyadic decompositions, and the class is closed under composition. The result is a multi-parameter theory that is intermediate between product theory and each of the one-parameter theories corresponding to the given homogeneities. In this paper we proceed with this type of analysis, introducing new ideas and techniques or adapting those available in product theory.

The contents of this paper is discussed in detail in Section
2
, but it may be useful to begin with a general description of the place of multi-norm theory in relation with other well established theories in Euclidean harmonic analysis having to do with dilations of different kinds. It is customary to distinguish between one-parameter and multi-parameter theories, the first being essentially based on Calderón-Zygmund theory and its extensions, while the second ones require more sophisticated techniques.

A one-parameter structure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is determined by a family of dilations,

\delta_{t}(\mathbf{x})=\delta_{t}(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=(t^{\lambda_{1}}x_{1},\dots,t^{\lambda_{d}}x_{d})

(1.1)

depending on a single parameter
t>0
.
The exponents
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d}
are assumed to be strictly positive, and we use the notation
\delta_{r}(\mathbf{x})=r\mathbf{x}
when no ambiguity with scalar multiplication is possible. The relevant ingredients of the theory are Calderón-Zygmund singular integrals, Fourier multipliers of Mihlin-Hörmander type, Hardy-Littlewood maximal functions and their smoothened versions, Littlewood-Paley decompositions, square functions, and Hardy and BMO spaces. Each of these structures also admits a “local” sub-theory, which includes Calderón-Zygmund kernels with compact support and maximal functions restricted to small scaling parameters. On the Fourier transform side, the local theory includes Mihlin-Hörmander multipliers that are only singular at infinity, and Littlewood-Paley decompositions limited to high frequencies. The local Hardy spaces are the
\mathbf{h}^{p}
spaces introduced by D. Goldberg
[
17
,
18
]
.

The prototypical examples of multi-parameter theory are the
product structures
. In this paper, an
n
-parameter product structure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, endowed with dilations of the form (
1.1
), is obtained by decomposing
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as product of dilation-invariant subspaces
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}},\ldots,\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
and then allowing independent scaling in each component as in
[
12
]
:

\delta_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})=\big(t_{1}\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\mathbf{x}_{n})\ ,\qquad(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\ .

(1.2)

The corresponding product theory includes singular integral operators of product type (such as tensor products of Calderón-Zygmund kernels on each component), strong-type maximal functions, Marcinkiewicz multipliers, and the product Hardy and
BMO
spaces of A. Chang and R. Fefferman
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
. Product theory also admits
local
versions; see
[
10
,
11
]
for the relevant local Hardy spaces. In these theories it is also of interest to determine the precise order of regularity of integral kernels and multipliers required to obtain boundedness results. However in this paper we will only consider kernels and multipliers that are smooth away from the appropriate singular set (the origin in the one-parameter setting, the coordinate hyperplanes in the product setting, etc.) and satisfy the appropriate differential inequalities for derivatives of any order.

A basic aspect of these theories that identifies the structure at hand and serves as a paradigm for extensions to new ones is the
dyadic Littlewood-Paley decomposition
. This is the
spectral partition
of the frequency space
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
into bounded sets
B_{L}
depending on a scale parameter
L
varying in an appropriate subset
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. In the
n
-parameter product theory defined by the dilations (
1.2
), these sets
B_{L}
depend on
L=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and one can take
B_{L}=\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}:2^{\ell_{i}-1}<|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\leq 2^{\ell_{i}}\ \forall\,i\big\}
. (With
n=1
this also covers the one-parameter case.) For the local product theory, the parameter
L
varies in
\mathbb{N}^{n}
and
B_{L}=\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:1+|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\sim 2^{\ell_{i}}\ \forall\,i\big\}
. The Littlewood-Paley decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}}B_{L}
associated to a given structure is essentially unique and identifies the structure. The following analytic objects can be defined in terms of it.

(i)

The associated
Littlewood-Paley square functions
, of the form
Sf(\mathbf{x})=\big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\big|P_{L}f(\mathbf{x})\big|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
where
\sum_{L}P_{L}=I
and each
P_{L}
is a smoothened spectral projection on
B_{L}
. The simplest example is that of an operator
\widehat{P_{L}f}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=\widehat{f}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\eta_{L}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
, where
\{\eta_{L}\}
is a smooth partition of unity with each
\eta_{L}
supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
B_{L}
and satisfying appropriate uniformity conditions.

(ii)

The algebra (under point-wise product) of Fourier multipliers
m({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
adapted to the given structure, which can be expressed as
\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}m_{L}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
, with smooth functions
m_{L}
satisfying the same support and uniformity condition as the
\eta_{L}
in (i).

(iii)

The convolution algebra of singular kernels
\mathcal{K}
adapted to the given structure, consisting of the inverse Fourier transforms of the multipliers in (ii).

(iv)

The Hardy spaces
\mathbf{H}^{p}
(or
\mathbf{h}^{p}
if the structure is local) with
p\leq 1
, consisting of those distributions
f
whose square functions
Sf
, as defined in (i), is in
L^{p}
.

For one-parameter and product structures, (i)-(iv) describe well-known objects. Multi-norm structures depend on the decomposition of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as the product of
n
subspaces
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
and on the choice of an
n\times n

standard matrix

\mathbf{E}
, as defined below in (
2.4
). The most interesting property of this structure, and the reason for its study, is that it describes, under appropriate non-degeneracy conditions, the singular integral operators arising as compositions of Calderón-Zygmund operators adapted to
n
different dilation families. A brief history of this study is the following.
A parametrix for the
\overline{\partial}
-Neumann problem on strictly pseudo-convex domain was constructed in
[
19
]
, and the resulting operators involve compositions of two kinds of operators that reflect the co-existence of two geometries on the boundary of these domains: the “isotropic” Riemannian geometry induced from the ambient space and the “parabolic” sub-Riemannian geometry induced by the CR-structure. These operators have different homogeneities, and one of the “open questions” from 1979 raised by N. Rivière (collected in
[
34
, p. xvii]
) concerns the behavior near
p=1
of the composition of two Calderón-Zygmund operators on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, one elliptic and the other parabolic. Some years later this question was studied by D.H. Phong and E.M. Stein in
[
30
,
31
]
(questions related to this have been studied recently by Han, Krantz, and Tan
[
20
]
). In
[
29
]
, A. Nagel, F. Ricci, E.M. Stein and S. Wainger described the classes of kernels and Fourier multipliers that are obtained by composing
n
local Calderón-Zygmund operators for
n
dilation families depending on a standard matrix. The differential inequalities satisfied by these operators involve
n
different homogeneous norms, generated by the assignment of the matrix
\mathbf{E}
. The two-factor situation is simpler than those with a higher number of factors. For instance, in the definition of the standard matrix
\mathbf{E}
in (
2.4
) below, if
n=2
the conditions in the second line are essentially vacuous and the strict inequality in the third line is verified as soon as the two chosen dilations are different, modulo a possible interchange. This is no longer true for
n\geq 3
, as shown by a comparison between the cases
n=2
and
n=3
described in Section
3.6
.

Multi-norm structures are defined as local structures. We stress from the beginning that this is not an arbitrary choice, but arises naturally from direct analysis of compositions of singular kernels or Littlewood-Paley decompositions.
Each multi-norm structure is, in any reasonable sense, intermediate between the local product structure for the decomposition of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as product of the
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
and any of the
n
local one-parameter structures induced by the dilations dictated by the matrix
\mathbf{E}
. For instance, the multi-norm Littlewood-Paley decomposition of the frequency space is coarser than the product decomposition and finer than each of the
n
one-parameter ones. Similarly, the space of multi-norm Fourier multipliers is included in the space of local Marcinkiewicz multipliers and includes the
n
spaces of local Mihlin-Hörmander multipliers, etc.

Another kind of structure,
flag structure
, has been introduced in recent years, and this is also intermediate between one-parameter and product structures.
Multi-norm and flag structures have many points in common, but there are also several differences. The common setting is
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, endowed with the dilations (
1.1
) and decomposed as product of dilation invariant subspaces
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
. The typical example of a
flag singular kernel
is a convolution
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}*\cdots*\mathcal{K}_{n}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is a smooth Calderón-Zygmund kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
adapted to the given dilations and, for
i\geq 2
,
\mathcal{K}_{i}=\delta_{\mathbf{0}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{i}
,
\delta_{\mathbf{0}}
denoting the Dirac delta on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{i-1}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{i}
a smooth Calderón-Zygmund kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
, also adapted to the given dilations. See
[
25
]
,
[
26
]
, where flag kernels appear for the first time (though not with this name),
[
28
]
,
[
27
]
for formal definitions and analysis of singular kernels and multipliers, and
[
23
,
35
,
21
,
6
]
for introduction and study of flag Hardy spaces. In the last set of references, however, the analysis is limited to the two-factor case. See also
[
15
,
16
]
for a different approach to flag theory on nilpotent groups. To the best of our knowledge the literature on multi-norm structures consists, in addition to the already mentioned papers, of
[
22
,
36
]
, both limited to two factors.

The objective of this paper is to develop and extend parts of multi-norm theory not treated in
[
29
]
, concentrating our attention on the definition of a
multi-norm Littlewood-Paley decomposition
of the frequency space, on the equivalence of different forms of Littlewood-Paley square functions in defining a local Hardy space for
p=1
, and finally on the atomic structure of this space.
Our approach to multi-norm Littlewood-Paley theory is new, even in the two-factor case. Nonetheless, we prove that the Hardy space defined in this paper coincides with that, previously defined in
[
22
,
36
]
, with
n=2
and by means of other (non-multinorm) square functions.

2.
Basic definitions and outline of the paper

The underlying space in this paper is
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, endowed with the dilations
\delta_{t}
in (
1.1
).
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is decomposed as the product of
n
subspaces:
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
, and each
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
is invariant under
\delta_{t}
. There are two ‘dual’ copies of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
: convolution kernels live on the ‘physical space’
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbf{x}}^{d}
where variables are denoted by Latin letters
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y},\ldots
, while the corresponding multipliers live on the dual ‘frequency space’
\mathbb{R}_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}^{d}
where the variables are denoted by Greek letters
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
,
\boldsymbol{\eta},\ldots
. If
\mathbf{x},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
write
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})
and
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{n})
with
\mathbf{x}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
. To identify individual scalar coordinates, let
\big\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\big\}
be the partition of
\{1,\ldots,d\}
so that
\mathbf{x}_{i}=(x_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}
.
1
1

1

At a first stage the reader may assume that the subspaces
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
are one-dimensional and that the dilations
\delta_{t}
are just scalar multiplication by
t
, but later on must be prepared to meet coarser decompositions with non-isotropic dilations coming into the picture.
Thus

\displaystyle E_{i}=\Big\{h\in\{1,\ldots,d\}:\text{$x_{h}$ is a coordinate in $\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}$}\Big\}.

(2.1)

If
G\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
, we write
\mathbb{R}^{G}
,
\mathbf{x}_{G}
,
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{G}
for
\bigoplus_{i\in G}\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
,
(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i\in G}
,
({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i})_{i\in G},\textit{etc.}
. We denote by
G^{c}
the complement of
G
in
\{1,\dots,n\}
. For any
d
-tuple
{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})
we write
{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}_{i}=(\alpha_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}
. Denote the homogeneous dimensions of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
by

\displaystyle q_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{h\in E_{i}}\lambda_{h},

(2.2)

and by
|\mathbf{x}_{i}|
the homogeneous norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
given by

\displaystyle|\mathbf{x}_{i}|=\sum\nolimits_{h\in E_{i}}|x_{h}|^{\frac{1}{\lambda_{h}}}.

(2.3)

An
n\times n
matrix
\mathbf{E}
with positive entries
\{e(j,k)\}
is called
standard
if

\displaystyle e(j,j)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{for $1\leq j\leq n$},

(2.4)

\displaystyle e(j,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq e(j,k)e(k,\ell)

\displaystyle\text{for $1\leq j,k,\ell\leq n$},

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle<e(j,k)e(k,j)

\displaystyle\text{for $1\leq j,k\leq n$, $j\neq k$}.

In
[
29
]
, a matrix satisfying the first two conditions in (
2.4
) is said to satisfy the
basic assumptions
. Notice that the first two conditions imply that
1\leq e(i,j)e(j,i)
for all
i,j
. The purpose of the added third condition is to exclued a degeneracy as explained in Remarks
3.13
. Fixing a standard matrix
\mathbf{E}
, we define families of dilations
\delta_{t}^{i}(\mathbf{x})
and
\widehat{\delta}_{t}^{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
and corresponding homogeneous norms
N_{i}(\mathbf{x})
and
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
by

\displaystyle\delta_{t}^{i}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\big(t^{\frac{1}{e(i,1)}}\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,t^{\frac{1}{e(i,n)}}\mathbf{x}_{n}\big),

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}_{t}^{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})

\displaystyle=\big(t^{e(1,i)}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{1},\dots,t^{e(n,i)}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{n}\big),

(2.5)

\displaystyle N_{i}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\max\big\{|\mathbf{x}_{j}|^{e(i,j)}:1\leq j\leq n\big\},

\displaystyle\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})

\displaystyle=\max\big\{|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}|^{\frac{1}{e(j,i)}}:1\leq j\leq n\big\}.

This notation is taken from
[
29
]
.
2
2

2

The symbols
N
and
\widehat{N}
are motivated in
[
29
]
by the fact that the norms
N_{i}(\mathbf{x})
control the size of the singular multi-norm kernels and their derivatives, while the norms
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
control the size of derivatives of Fourier multipliers. We will see that the same occurs in this paper with the dyadic decompositions in the
\mathbf{x}
- and the
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
-space.
The choice of the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm for
N_{i}(\mathbf{x})
and
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
produces various technical simplifications. For instance, all the norms in (
2.5
) have the same unit ball given by
B(1)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}(1)=\big\{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\text{$|\mathbf{x}_{j}|\leq 1$ for $1\leq j\leq n$}\big\}
, and similarly in the
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
-variables.

2.1.
Littlewood-Paley decomposition of
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}

An important focus of this paper is the multi-norm Littlewood-Paley decomposition of frequency space
\mathbb{R}_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}^{d}
, and this is discussed in detail in the first four sections of Section
3
. Here we give a brief introduction. The starting point is a decomposition of the complement
B(1)^{c}
of the unit ball in the frequency space
\mathbb{R}_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}^{d}
. For
1\leq i\leq n
, the local one-parameter Littlewood-Paley partition of the frequency space associated to the
i
-th dilation
\widehat{\delta}^{i}_{r}
in (
2.5
) consists of the unit ball
B(1)=B_{{\mathbf{0}}}^{i}
and the dyadic annuli
B_{\ell}^{i}=\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\sim 2^{\ell}\big\}
with
\ell\geq 1
so that
\mathbb{R}_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}^{d}=B(1)\cup\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\infty}B_{\ell}^{i}
. Then, roughly speaking, the
multi-norm Littlewood-Paley decomposition
is the minimal decomposition that is finer than all the
\widehat{\delta}_{t}^{i}
Littlewood-Paley decompositions for
1\leq i\leq n
. It consists of the
non-empty
intersections

\displaystyle B_{L}=\bigcap\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}B^{j}_{\ell_{j}}=\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\mathbb{R}_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}^{d}:\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\sim 2^{\ell_{i}}\text{ if }\ell_{i}\geq 1\ ,\ \widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\leq 1\text{ if }\ell_{i}=0\Big\},

(2.6)

with
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
.
Thus the multi-norm Littlewood-Paley decomposition is indexed by the set
\mathcal{L}=\big\{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:B_{L}\neq\emptyset\big\}

of
admissible scales
.

To better understand the decomposition we introduce the notions of dominance and marked partitions.
We say that a component
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}
is
dominant
in the norm
\widehat{N}_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
if
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}|^{\frac{1}{e(j,i)}}
.
3
3

3

Two different components are both dominant only on a negligible set of
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
.
Then the inequalities (
2.4
) for a standard matrix imply that if
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}
is dominant in
\widehat{N}_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
then it is also dominant in
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
. It follows that dominant components in
\widehat{N}_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
are described by a
marked partition
,
i.e.
a partition
\{1,\ldots,n\}=A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{s}
together with a “marked entry”
k_{r}\in A_{r}
for
1\leq r\leq s
so that
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}
is dominant in
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
for all
i\in A_{r}
. We write such a marked partition as
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
. Given a marked partition
S
there is then the associated set of
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
such that the dominant components of
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
are given by
S
.

The
principal marked partition

S_{p}
has
s=n
and
A_{r}=\{r\}
. The associated
principal cone
is

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathbf{E})=\big\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}:\text{$t_{i}\leq e(i,j)t_{j}$ for $1\leq i,j\leq n$}\big\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}.

(2.7)

The elements
\mathbf{t}\in\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
are characterized by the following property: if
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is such that
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|=2^{t_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,n
, then every
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}
is dominant in
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
. It turns out that the index set
\mathcal{L}
consists of those lattice points
L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
at distance at most
1
from
\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
. More generally, associated to each marked partition
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
there is a cone
\Gamma_{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
(see (
3.5
)).

The explicit forms of the sets
B_{L}
with
L\in\mathcal{L}\setminus\{0\}
can also be described in terms of marked partitions. Roughly speaking, if
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})
is close to
\Gamma_{S}
then
B_{L}
can be expressed as the set of
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
with
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}|\sim 2^{\ell_{k_{r}}}
for
r=1,\dots,s
and, if
i\neq k_{1},\dots,k_{r}
, then
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\lesssim 2^{\ell_{i}}
.
This indicates that a bump function supported in a small neighborhood of
B_{L}
has an inverse Fourier transform with cancellation in the marked variables
\mathbf{x}_{k_{r}}
, but not in the others.

Thus the set
\mathcal{L}\setminus\{0\}
naturally decomposes, in an essentially unique way, as a disjoint union of subsets
\mathcal{L}_{S}
indexed by marked partitions
S
. However, only those
S
such that
\Gamma_{S}
has nonempty interior will play a role. We denote by
\mathcal{S}_{n}
the set of all marked partitions and by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
the set for which
\Gamma_{S}
has nonempty interior. A coarser decomposition of
\mathcal{L}
is obtained as the union of the
\mathcal{L}_{S}
for all the marked partitions
S
with the same set
D
of marked elements. In this case we use the notation
\mathcal{L}(D)
. It is then natural to set
\mathcal{L}(\emptyset)=\mathcal{L}_{\emptyset}=\{0\}
. We define
\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{E}}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}\cup\{\emptyset\}
, so that the most frequently used partition of
\mathcal{L}
takes the form

\mathcal{L}=\bigcup\nolimits_{S\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{E}}}\mathcal{L}_{S}.

(2.8)

Note that this Littlewood-Paley decomposition is
local
in nature; the sets
B_{L}
are all “large”, the associated singular integral kernels are singular only at the origin, and the associated multipliers are smooth at the origin. See Question 1 in Section
13
for more comments on this issue.

In Section
3.6
we describe in detail the Littlewood-Paley decomposition in the cases
n=2
or
n=3
since this may help the reader understand the geometry involved in the general construction.

2.2.
Littlewood-Paley and Calderón reproducing formulas

For
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
we set

\displaystyle f^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})=2^{\sum_{1}^{n}\ell_{j}q_{j}}f(2^{\ell_{1}}\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,2^{\ell_{n}}\mathbf{x}_{n}).

(2.9)

A
multi-norm Littlewood-Paley family

\{\Xi_{L}:L\in\mathcal{L}\}\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a set of uniformly bounded Schwartz functions such that
\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\Xi_{L}^{(-L)}=\delta_{\mathbf{0}}
in the distribution sense. A (discrete)
multi-norm Calderón family
associated to
\{\Xi_{L}:L\in\mathcal{L}\}
is then a set
\Xi=\big\{\Xi_{L},\,\widetilde{\Xi}_{L}:L\in\mathcal{L}\big\}\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm, such that
\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\widetilde{\Xi}_{L}^{(-L)}*\Xi_{L}^{(-L)}=\delta_{\mathbf{0}}
. We use the expression
Littlewood-Paley reproducing formula
and
Calderón reproducing formulas
for the identities
f=\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}f*\Xi_{L}^{(-L)}
and
f=\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}f*\Xi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Xi}_{L}^{(-L)}
respectively. In Section
4
we construct associated Calderón families whose tilded elements
\widetilde{\Xi}_{L}
have vanishing moments (depending on the type and on
L
) up to any preassigned order. Our proofs of Propositions
4.3
and
4.7
are based on the construction due to V. Rychkov
[
33
]
in the one-parameter case. These are based on two different types of Littlewood-Paley-families adapted to the multi-norm structure.

(i)
Tensor type.

For each
1\leq i\leq n
we take a local one-parameter reproducing formula
f(\mathbf{x}_{i})=\sum_{\ell_{i}=0}^{\infty}f*\psi_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{j})}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
where
\psi_{i,\ell_{i}}
are uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}})
and have integral zero for
\ell\geq 1
. Taking the tensor product
\Psi_{L}^{\otimes}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})=\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\psi_{i,\ell_{i}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
we obtain a reproducing formula
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}f*(\Psi_{L}^{\otimes})^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but this is not a Littlewood-Paley reproducing formula in our sense since we are summing over
L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
rather than
L\in\mathcal{L}
. However, we can easily obtain a Littlewood-Paley reproducing formula
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})
by appropriately grouping together finite sets of indices. Each
\Psi_{L}
will have integral zero in any variable
\mathbf{x}_{i}
with
i\in D_{L}
. The same can be applied to the
n
Calderón reproducing formulas, producing a multi-norm Calderón reproducing formula
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})
, where each
\Psi_{L}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}
is a tensor product and each
\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}
has vanishing moments in any variable
\mathbf{x}_{i}
with
i\in D_{L}
.

(ii)
Convolution type.

For each
1\leq i\leq n
we construct a reproducing formula on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
using the
i^{th}
family
\widehat{\delta}_{t}^{i}
of dilations defined in (
2.5
). With the notation
f^{(-\ell)_{i}}(\mathbf{x})=2^{\ell\sum_{j}e(j,i)q_{j}}f(\widehat{\delta}^{i}_{2^{\ell}}\mathbf{x})
, we fix functions
\sigma_{i,\ell}
with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with integral zero if
\ell\geq 1
and giving a reproducing formula
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}f*\sigma_{i,\ell}^{(-\ell)_{i}}(\mathbf{x})
. By composition, we obtain a reproducing formula
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}f*\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}}^{(-\ell_{1})_{1}}*\cdots*\sigma_{n,\ell_{n}}^{(-\ell_{n})_{n}}(\mathbf{x})
which, once again, is not a multi-norm Littlewood-Paley decomposition. However, the same grouping of terms with
L\not\in\mathcal{L}
used in (i) produces a multi-norm reproducing formula
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}f*\Sigma_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})
. It is proved in Corollary
4.6
that the terms
\Sigma_{L}^{(-L)}
produced by this grouping can be expressed as convolutions
\sigma_{1,\ell_{1}}^{(-\ell_{1})_{1}}*\cdots*\sigma_{n,\ell_{n}}^{(-\ell_{n})_{n}}
, where the
\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}
are uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and have integral zero if
i\in D_{L}
. The same operations can be performed on Calderón reproducing formulas relative to the
n
dilation families to obtain a multi-norm Calderón reproducing formula of convolution type, with the
\widetilde{\sigma}_{i,\ell_{i}}
uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and with vanishing moments up to any preassigned order in each variable
i\in D_{L}
.

2.3.
Square functions

Given a Littlewood-Paley family
\Xi=\big\{\Xi_{L}:L\in\mathcal{L}\big\}
, we define the associated square function

\mathcal{S}_{\Xi}f=\Big(\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\big|f*\Xi_{L}^{(-L)}\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.10)

One of the main themes of this paper is the
L^{1}
-equivalence
of certain multi-norm square functions, and this is the subject of Sections
5
,
6
, and
7.5
. Two square functions
S_{\Xi}
and
S_{\Xi^{\prime}}
are
L^{1}
-equivalent if the condition
S_{\Xi}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is equivalent to
S_{\Xi^{\prime}}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every tempered distribution
f
. All the different versions of square functions will play a role in the description of the multi-norm Hardy space
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In Section
5
, Theorem
5.1
, we prove
L^{1}
-equivalence of square functions of tensor type. In Section
6.1
, Theorem
6.2
, we prove
L^{1}
-equivalence of square functions of convolution type and then, in Theorem
6.4
, between these and those of tensor type. In Section
6.3
we study the operator
S^{\prime\prime}_{\sigma}f=\big(\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\big|f*\big(\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\big)\big|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
which arises from the reproducing formula
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{L\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}f*\big(\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\big)
mentioned above and, in Theorem
6.10
, prove its
L^{1}
-equivalence with the previous square functions.
4
4

4

This is not a Littlewood-Paley decomposition because it is not necessarily true that each term is an approximate spectral projection. This type of square function, however, is a local analogue of the ones used in previous work on Hardy spaces related to the two-norm situation
[
6
]
. It is also useful in some proofs where an inductive argument on the number
n
of factors is needed.
In Theorem
7.7
in Section
7.5
we establish
L^{1}
-equivalence of
Plancherel-Pólya
variants of the square functions of tensor type. The definition of these requires the notion of multi-norm dyadic rectangles, which is the subject of Section
7
.

2.4.
Dyadic structure of
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}
and Plancherel-Pólya square functions

In Section
7
we shift our attention to the
\mathbf{x}
-space and define multi-norm dyadic cubes and rectangles. This requires some care since several different families of dilations are involved. These definitions are “local” in the sense that we are only concerned with sets at scales smaller than
1
, and are adapted the the norms
\{N_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}
. These notions are used in Section
7.5
to define Plancherel-Pólya type square functions, and in Section
12
, in connection with the atomic theory of the Hardy space
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Dyadic cubes
\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}
at scales
2^{-\ell_{i}}
are defined in each factor
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, and dyadic rectangles
\mathbf{R}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are products of dyadic cubes
\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
at scales
2^{-\ell_{i}}
subject to the admissibility condition
L=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
.

2.5.
Local Hardy space

In Section
8
we introduce the multi-norm Hardy space
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the space of distributions
f
such that, for any of the square functions
S_{\Xi}
introduced in the previous sections,
S_{\Xi}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We first prove that
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is contained in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(Theorem
8.1
). Proposition
8.4
shows that it is invariant under smooth cutoffs and consequently that every
f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be decomposed as a sum of
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}
-functions supported in a mesh of unit cubes with control of their norms. Theorem
8.6
shows that the multi-norm singular integral operators (defined in Section
3.5
) are bounded from
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to itself. Theorem
8.9
shows that
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can also be defined via
multi-norm local Riesz tranforms

R_{J}=\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{1,j_{1}}*\cdots*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{n,j_{n}}
, where for each fixed
i=1,\dots,n
, the kernels
\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{i,j}
characterize the local Hardy space
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\widehat{\delta}^{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
relative to the dilations
\widehat{\delta}^{i}
.

2.6.
Atomic characterization of
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

In Section
9
we obtain an atomic decomposition of
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}
-functions. Atoms depend on a parameter
\tau>0
and an element
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}
. There are three types, depending on the number
s
of marked indices in
S
.
The precise definition of an
(S,\tau)
-atom is given in Section
9.1
, the following being only a rough description.

1.

If
s=0
then
S=\emptyset
and an
(\emptyset,\tau)
-atom is a function supported in a translate of
2^{\tau}\mathbb{B}(1)
and normalized in
L^{2}
, with no required cancellation.

2.

If
s=1
with dotted entry
k
, an
(S,\tau)
-atom
a
is a Coifman-Weiss atom for the local Hardy space
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\delta^{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
associated to the dilations
\delta^{k}
in (
2.5
), supported on the
2^{\tau}
-dilate of a dyadic cube at scale
2^{-L}
, with
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
, satisfying the stronger cancellation
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}}}a(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}_{k}=0
.

3.

If
S=\big\{(A_{r},k_{r}):r=1,\dots,s\big\}
with
s\geq 2
, an
(S,\tau)
-atom is a product (Chang-Fefferman) atom relative to the coarser decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{A_{1}}\times\cdots\mathbb{R}^{A_{s}}
with dilations
\delta^{k_{r}}
on each
A_{r}
, supported in a set
\Omega
contained in a translate of
\tau\mathbb{B}(1)
, with pre-atoms
b_{j}
, each supported on the
2^{\tau}
-dilate of a dyadic cube at scale
2^{-L_{j}}
, with
L_{j}\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
, with the stronger cancellations
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{k_{r}}}}b_{j}(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}_{k_{r}}=0
for every
r=1,\dots,s
.

Theorem
9.3
gives an atomic decomposition of a function
f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
: for any
\tau>0
, there is a constant
C_{\tau}
so that
f=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
,
where each
a_{j}
is a
(S_{j},\tau)
-atom for some
S_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}
and
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}|\lambda_{j}|\leq C_{\tau}\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
.
More precisely, rewriting the Calderón reproducing formula as

f=f*\Psi_{0}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{0}+\sum\nolimits_{S\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{E}}}\Big(\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}\Big)=f_{\emptyset}+\sum\nolimits_{S\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{E}}}f_{S},

the proof shows that for given
\tau
, each term
f_{S}
decomposes as a sum of
(S,\tau)
-atoms.

To complete the discussion of atomic decompositions, it remains to show that every
(S,\tau)
-atom belongs to the space
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The proof is given in Theorem
12.1
and Proposition
12.2
, and requires a preliminary estimate on convolutions with multi-norm kernels proved in Section
11
as well as a version of Journé’s covering lemma in
n
-parameters. The required statements are stated and proved in Section
10
, and follows the arguments in
[
2
]
.

3.
Multi-norm structures

In this section we review the basic multi-norm theory developed in
[
29
]
. Write
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
and let
\mathbf{E}=\{e(i,j)\}
be a standard
n\times n
matrix as defined in (
2.4
). The family of dilations
\delta_{r}
is defined in (
1.1
), and
q_{i}=\sum_{h\in E_{i}}\lambda_{i}
is the homogeneous dimensions of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
as in (
2.2
). For
1\leq i\leq n
,
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|
is the
\delta_{r}
–homogeneous norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
as in (
2.3
).
Let
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}=\big\{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\text{$t_{j}\geq 0$ for $1\leq j\leq n$}\}
. For
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}),\,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, recall from (
2.5
) that

\displaystyle\delta_{t}^{i}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\big(t^{\frac{1}{e(i,1)}}\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,t^{\frac{1}{e(i,n)}}\mathbf{x}_{n}\big),

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}_{t}^{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})

\displaystyle=\big(t^{e(1,i)}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{1},\dots,t^{e(n,i)}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{n}\big),

(3.1)

\displaystyle N_{i}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\max\nolimits_{1\leq j\leq n}|\mathbf{x}_{j}|^{e(i,j)},

\displaystyle\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})

\displaystyle=\max\nolimits_{1\leq j\leq n}|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}|^{\frac{1}{e(j,i)}}.

To obtain the explicit description of the sets
\mathcal{L}
and
B_{L}
in (
2.6
), it is convenient to introduce logarithmic parameters
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}=\big\{\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:t_{i}\geq 0\ \forall i\big\}
and logarithmic norms
\widehat{n}_{i}(t)
by setting

\mathbf{t}=\pi({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\overset{\rm def}{=}\left(\log_{2}^{+}|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{1}|,\ldots,\log_{2}^{+}|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{n}|\right)\text{ and }\widehat{n}_{i}(\mathbf{t})=\widehat{n}_{i}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=\max\nolimits_{1\leq j\leq n}|t_{j}|e(j,i)^{-1}.

(3.2)

The above formula for
\widehat{n}_{i}
is such that
\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=2^{\widehat{n}_{i}(\pi({\boldsymbol{\xi}}))}
for all
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in B(1)^{c}
,
The conditions (
2.4
) guarantee that the principal cone
\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
in (
2.7
) has nonempty interior. In the next section we privilege definitions in the
\mathbf{t}
variables, transporting them to the
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
variables via
\pi
.

3.1.
Marked partitions and dominance

A
marked partition

S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
is a partition of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
with the entries
k_{r}\in A_{r}
for
1\leq r\leq s
. We denote the set of
marked
, or
dotted
, elements of
S
as

\displaystyle D(S)=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}\}.

(3.3)

When convenient we can use a different notation for marked partitions. For instance, instead of writing
S=\big\{(\{1,3\},1),(\{2\},2)\big\}
we write
S=\{\overset{.}{1},3\}\{\overset{.}{2}\}
. The
principal marked partition
is

\displaystyle S_{p}=\{\overset{.}{1}\}\{\overset{.}{2}\}\cdots\{\overset{.}{n}\}.

(3.4)

The variable
t_{k}
is called
dominant
(respectively
strictly dominant
) in
\widehat{n}_{i}
at the point
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, or shortly “in
\widehat{n}_{i}(\mathbf{t})
”, if
\frac{t_{k}}{e(j,k)}\geq\frac{t_{j}}{e(j,i)}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
(respectively
\frac{t_{k}}{e(j,k)}>\frac{t_{j}}{e(j,i)}
for all
j\neq k
). If
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
is a marked partition, let

\displaystyle\Gamma_{S}=\Big\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}:\frac{t_{j}}{e(j,i)}\leq\frac{t_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\text{ for all $1\leq r\leq s$, all $i\in A_{r}$ and all $1\leq j\leq n$}\Big\}

(3.5)

denote the closed convex cone of points
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
such that
t_{k_{r}}
is dominant in
\widehat{n}_{i}(\mathbf{t})
for all
i\in A_{r}
and all
1\leq r\leq s
. Then
\Gamma_{S_{p}}=\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
. The symbols
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{E}}
are as defined just before equation (
2.8
). The notions of dominance and strict dominance are extended to the
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
variable in terms of the norms
\widehat{N}_{j}
as follows.

\widehat{E}_{S}=\pi^{-1}(\Gamma_{S})\cap B(1)^{c}=\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in B(1)^{c}:\text{ $j\in A_{r}\Longrightarrow|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\ell}|^{1/e(\ell,j)}\leq|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}|^{1/e(k_{r},j)}$ for $1\leq\ell\leq n$}\big\}.

(3.6)

It is possible for more than one variable to be dominant in the same norm. This occurs on the sets

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\big(\exists j\big)\big(\exists k\big)(\exists\ell\big)\big(\text{$\ell\neq k$ and $t_{k}e(k,j)^{-1}=t_{\ell}e(\ell,j)^{-1}$}\Big\},

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}

\displaystyle=\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(1):\big(\exists j\big)\big(\exists k\neq\ell\big)\big(|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k}|^{1/e(k,j)}=|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\ell}|^{1/e(\ell,j)}\big)\big\}.

These are finite unions of proper closed submanifolds and so have measure zero. Thus if
\mathbf{t}\notin\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(respectively
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\notin\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
), for every
j
there is a unique index
k
so that
t_{k}
is strictly dominant in
\widehat{n}_{j}(\mathbf{t})
(respectively
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k}
is strictly dominant in
\widehat{N}_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
), and the same is true on a full neighborhood of
\mathbf{t}
or
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
. Lemma
3.1
below summarizes the main consequences of these notions. The proofs can be extracted from Sections 3.1-3.3 in
[
29
]
.
5
5

5

Parts (
1
)–(
4
) only require the weaker hypothesis that the matrix satisfies the basic assumptions, the first two lines of (
2.4
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
satisfy (
2.4
) and let
S
be a marked partition.

(1)

If
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
and
t_{k}
is (strictly) dominant in
\widehat{n}_{j}(\mathbf{t})
for some
j\neq k
, then
t_{k}
is also (strictly) dominant in
\widehat{n}_{k}(\mathbf{t})
.

(2)

If
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in B(1)^{c}
and
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k}
is (strictly) dominant in
\widehat{N}_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
then
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k}
is (strictly) dominant in
\widehat{N}_{k}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
.

(3)

If
\mathbf{t}\notin\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
(respectively
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\notin\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
), there is a unique marked partition
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\dots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
such that, for every
r
,
t_{k_{r}}
is strictly dominant in
\widehat{n}_{j}(\mathbf{t})
for
j\in A_{r}
(respectively
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}
is strictly dominant in all the norms
\widehat{N}_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
with
j\in A_{r}
).

(4)

{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\widehat{E}_{S}
if and only if
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}
is dominant in
\widehat{N}_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
for all
j\in A_{r}
and every
1\leq r\leq s
.

(5)

If
\mathbf{E}
is a standard matrix and
S_{p}=\{\overset{.}{1}\}\{\overset{.}{2}\}\cdots\{\overset{.}{n}\}
then
\text{\rm Int}(\Gamma_{S_{p}})\neq\emptyset
.

Let
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\dots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
and let
1\leq q,r\leq s
. Define

\displaystyle\tau_{S}(k_{q},k_{r})=\min\nolimits_{\ell\in A_{r}}\frac{e(k_{q},\ell)}{e(k_{r},\ell)}.

(3.7)

The following result is contained in
[
29
]
, Corollary 3.10.

Proposition 3.2
.

If
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\dots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
then

\displaystyle\Gamma_{S}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}:t_{j}\leq\frac{t_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},j)}\text{ for all }j\in A_{r}\text{ and }t_{k_{p}}\leq\tau_{S}(k_{p},k_{q})t_{k_{q}}\text{ for all }1\leq p,q\leq s\Big\},

(3.8)

and

\displaystyle\widehat{E}_{S}=\Bigg\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in B(1)^{c}:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}|\leq|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}|^{\frac{1}{e(k_{r},j)}}&\text{ for }1\leq r\leq s\text{ and }j\in A_{r},\\
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{p}}|\leq|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{q}}|^{\tau_{S}(k_{p},k_{q})}&\text{ for }1\leq p,q\leq s\end{cases}\Bigg\}.

(3.9)

Remark 3.3
.

Notice that the conditions in (
3.9
) describing the set
\widehat{E}_{S}
can also be expressed in the following equivalent form:

\widehat{E}_{S}=\Bigg\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in B(1)^{c}:\begin{cases}N_{k_{r}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}})=|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}|&\text{ for }1\leq r\leq s,\\
N_{k_{p}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{p}})\leq N_{k_{q}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{q}})^{\tau_{S}(k_{p},k_{q})}&\text{ for }1\leq p,q\leq s\end{cases}\Bigg\},

(3.10)

where
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}}=({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i})_{i\in A_{r}}
. Together with the analogous reformulation of (
3.8
), this is interesting in two ways:

\bullet

it emphasizes the presence of a coarser multi-norm structure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
associated to
S
, with
\mathbb{R}^{d}
decomposed as
\prod_{r=1}^{s}\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
, homogeneous norms
N_{k_{r}}
, and principal cone
\big\{(t_{k_{1}},\dots,t_{k_{s}}):(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\Gamma_{S}\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{s}_{+}
;

\bullet

it gives a simple example of the interplay between the two kinds of norms in (
3.1
): starting from the dominance principle in the
\widehat{N}
-norms, the resulting sets
\widehat{E}_{S}
are described in terms of the
N
-norms.

The most relevant form of this interplay is the correspondence, via Fourier transform, described in Section
3.5
between kernels satisfying estimates involving the
N
-norms and multipliers satisfying estimates involving the
\widehat{N}
-norms.

3.2.
Partition of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
in terms of dotted elements

At this point it is preferable to classify points
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
in terms of sets
D
of dotted elements rather than in terms of marked partitions. By Lemma
3.4
below, it turns out that this can be done in a natural way without excluding the singular points in
\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. In fact we show that for every
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
the set of the
D(S)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
such that
\mathbf{t}\in\Gamma_{S}
admits a minimum element under inclusion.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
{\mathbf{0}}\neq\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
, let

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{t})=\big\{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}:\mathbf{t}\in\Gamma_{S}\big\}
. There exists a (possibly non-unique)
6
6

6

Non-uniqueness is quite possible if
n\geq 3
: Figure 2 shows that there are points belonging to
\Gamma_{S}\cap\Gamma_{S^{\prime}}
with
S=\{\overset{\cdot}{1}\}\{\overset{\cdot}{2},3\}
and
S^{\prime}=\{\overset{\cdot}{1},3\}\{\overset{\cdot}{2}\}
, with
D=\{1,2\}
.

S_{0}=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\dots,(A_{s_{0}},k_{s_{0}})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{t})
such that

(a)

t_{k_{p}}<e(k_{p},k_{q})t_{k_{q}}
for all
1\leq p\neq q\leq s_{0}
;

(b)

if
T=\big\{(B_{1},m_{1}),\ldots,(B_{\sigma},m_{\sigma})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{t})
then
\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{s_{0}}\}\subseteq\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\sigma}\big\}
.

Proof.

Let
s_{0}=\min\big\{\big|(D(S)\big|:S\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbf{t}})\big\}
, let
S_{0}=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\dots,(A_{s_{0}},k_{s_{0}})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{t})
, and suppose that
t_{k_{p}}\geq e(k_{p},k_{q})t_{k_{q}}
for some
p\neq q
. Now if
j\in A_{q}
,
\frac{t_{\ell}}{e(\ell,j)}\leq\frac{t_{k_{q}}}{e(k_{q},j)}
for every
\ell
since
\mathbf{t}\in S_{0}
and so

\displaystyle\frac{t_{\ell}}{e(\ell,j)}\leq\frac{t_{k_{q}}}{e(k_{q},j)}\leq\frac{t_{k_{p}}}{e(k_{p},k_{q})e(k_{q},j)}\leq\frac{t_{k_{p}}}{e(k_{p},j)}.

It then follows from (
3.5
) that
\mathbf{t}\in\Gamma_{\widetilde{S}_{0}}
where
\widetilde{S}_{0}
is obtained from
S_{0}
by replacing the two elements
(A_{p},k_{p}),(A_{q},k_{q})
of
S_{0}
with the single element
(A_{p}\cup A_{q},k_{p})
. This would contradict the minimality of
s_{0}
, proving (
a
).

Now let
T=\big\{(B_{1},m_{1}),\dots,(B_{s},m_{s})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbf{t}})
. To prove (
b
)
suppose that
k_{1}\notin\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\sigma}\}
. Without loss of generality, we may suppose that
k_{1}\in B_{1}\setminus\{m_{1}\}
. Let
p
be such that
m_{1}\in A_{p}
. If
p=1
then since
k_{1}\in B_{1}
, this means that
t_{m_{1}}\leq\frac{t_{k_{1}}}{e(k_{1},m_{1})}\leq\frac{t_{m_{1}}}{e(k_{1},m_{1})e(m_{1},k_{1})}
, implying that
e(k_{1},m_{1})e(m_{1},k_{1})\leq 1
. This contradicts the hypothesis that the matrix
\mathbf{E}
is standard, and so
p\neq 1
. It remains to consider the case
p\geq 2
. Suppose that
m_{1}\in A_{2}
. For every
i\in A_{1}
we have

t_{i}\leq\frac{t_{k_{1}}}{e(k_{1},i)}\leq\frac{t_{m_{1}}}{e(m_{1},k_{1})e(k_{1},i)}\leq\frac{t_{k_{2}}}{e(k_{2},m_{1})e(m_{1},k_{1})e(k_{1},i)}\leq\frac{t_{k_{2}}}{e(k_{2},i)}\ .

This implies that
S^{\prime}=\big\{(A_{1}\cup A_{2},k_{2}),(A_{3},k_{3}),\dots,(A_{s_{0}},k_{s_{0}})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbf{t}})
, contradicting once again minimality of
s_{0}
. This completes the proof.
∎

We denote by
D(\mathbf{t})
the set
D(S_{0})
,
S_{0}
being as in Lemma
3.4
. For
D\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
we define

\Gamma(D)=\begin{cases}\{0\}&\text{ if }D=\emptyset\\
\big\{\mathbf{t}\neq 0:D(\mathbf{t})=D\big\}&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Obviously the sets
\Gamma(D)
partition
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. The next result follows directly from Lemma
3.4
.

Proposition 3.5
.

For
\emptyset\neq D\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
\mathcal{S}(D)
be the set of
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
such that
D(S)=D
. Then

\Gamma(D)=\Big(\bigcup\nolimits_{S\in\mathcal{S}(D)}\Gamma_{S}\Big)\cap\Big\{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}:\text{$0<t_{k_{p}}<t_{k_{q}}e(k_{p},k_{q})$ for all $k_{p},k_{q}\in D$ with $p\neq q$}\Big\}.

The sets
\Gamma(D)
are cones but are not necessarily convex. If
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
and
D=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}\}
let
F_{S}=\Gamma_{S}\cap\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
and
F(D)=\Gamma(D)\cap\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
so that

\displaystyle F_{S}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}:\text{$t_{j}=\frac{t_{k_{p}}}{e(k_{p},j)}$ if $j\in A_{r}$ and $t_{k_{p}}\leq\tau_{S}(k_{p},k_{q})t_{k_{q}}$ for $1\leq p,q\leq s$}\Big\},

(3.11)

\displaystyle F(D)

\displaystyle=\Big(\bigcup\nolimits_{S\in\mathcal{S}(D)}F_{S}\Big)\cap\left\{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}:t_{k_{p}}<e(k_{p},k_{q})t_{k_{q}}\text{ for all\ }k_{p}\neq k_{q}\in D\right\}.

In particular if
S_{p}
is the principal marked partition from (
3.4
) then
F_{S_{\rm p}}=\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
. The sets
\big\{F(D):D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\big\}
are disjoint by Proposition
3.5
, and partition the cone
\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
. More precisely, if
D=\{1,\dots,n\}
,
F(D)=\text{\rm Int\,}\big(\Gamma(\mathbf{E})\big)
, and the faces
F(D)
with
D\subsetneqq\{1,\dots,n\}
partition
\partial\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
, with
F(\emptyset)=\{0\}
.
7
7

7

It can be proved that each set
F_{S}
is a full
face
of
\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
, but we do not prove this since it is not needed. If
n\geq 3
not all faces of
\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
are included.

3.3.
Multi-norm dyadic decomposition of
\mathbb{R}_{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}^{d}

If
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and
D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
let

\displaystyle Q_{L}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\ell_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\leq s_{j}\leq\ell_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{D}

\displaystyle=\Big\{Q_{L}:L\in\Gamma(D)\Big\}.

(3.12)

The sets
Q_{L}
are cubes of unit size with disjoint interiors and are entirely contained in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
if all
\ell_{j}>0
.
Since the sets
\big\{\Gamma(D):D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\big\}
partition
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
, the sets
\big\{\mathcal{Q}_{D}:D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\big\}
partition the set of cubes
\{Q_{L}:L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\}
. Let
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}\}
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
be non-empty.
The set
Q_{L}\in\mathcal{Q}_{D}
is
maximal in
\mathcal{Q}_{D}
if
Q_{L+\mathbf{e}_{j}}\notin\mathcal{Q}_{D}
for every
j\not\in D
. Note that if
D=\{1,\ldots,n\}
every
Q_{L}\in\mathcal{Q}_{D}
is automatically maximal in
\mathcal{Q}_{D}
and
L\in\text{\rm Int\,}\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
. Also if
D=\emptyset
then
\mathcal{Q}(\emptyset)
contains exactly the unit cube centered at the origin, which is obviously maximal in
\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset}
. For
\emptyset\neq D\subsetneqq\{1,\ldots,n\}
the situation is more complicated.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\emptyset\neq D\subsetneqq\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
L\in\Gamma(D)
and
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
be a marked partition with
D(S)=D
and
L\in\Gamma(D)\cap\Gamma_{S}
. Let
\lfloor x\rfloor
denote the largest integer less than or equal to
x
. With
\tau_{S}(k_{p},k_{q})
defined in (
3.7
), if
L\in\Gamma_{S}
then
Q_{L}
is maximal in
\mathcal{Q}_{D}
if and only if

\displaystyle\ell_{i}

\displaystyle=\left\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\right\rfloor

\displaystyle\text{for all $i\in A_{r}$}

\displaystyle\text{and}

\displaystyle\ell_{k_{p}}

\displaystyle<\tau_{S}(k_{p},k_{q})\ell_{k_{q}}

\displaystyle\text{for all $1\leq p\neq q\leq s$}.

(3.13)

Proof.

The conditions (
3.13
) are necessary in general and also sufficient is there is no other
S^{\prime}
such that
D(S^{\prime})=D
and
L\in\Gamma(D)\cap\Gamma_{S^{\prime}}
. Assuming instead that this occurs with
S^{\prime}=\big\{(B_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(B_{s},k_{s})\big\}
, suppose
i\in B_{q}\cap A_{r}
. Then both
\ell_{k_{r}}
and
\ell_{k_{q}}
are dominant in
\widehat{n}_{i}(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})
and it follows from (
3.1
) that
e(k_{r},i)^{-1}\ell_{k_{r}}=e(k_{q},i)^{-1}\ell_{k_{q}}
. So the conditions (
3.13
) are the same for
S
and
S^{\prime}
.
∎

Let
D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
and put

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(D)

\displaystyle=\Big\{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:\text{$Q_{L}$ is maximal in $\mathcal{Q}_{D}$}\Big\}

\displaystyle\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\nolimits_{D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mathcal{L}(D)\subset\mathbb{N}^{n}.

(3.14)

Since
\{\mathcal{Q}_{D}\}
partitions the set of cubes,
\{\mathcal{L}(D):D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\big\}
is a partition of the index set
\mathcal{L}
. If
L\in\mathcal{L}
let
D_{L}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
be the unique subset such that
L\in\Gamma(D_{L})
. We enlarge each cube which is maximal in
\mathcal{Q}_{D}
to a larger
tube
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
. If
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
put

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{L}

\displaystyle=\Big\{L^{\prime}=(\ell_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\ell_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:\text{$\ell_{j}^{\prime}=\ell_{j}$ if $j\in D_{L}$ and $0\leq\ell_{j}^{\prime}\leq\ell_{j}$ if $j\notin D_{L}$}\Big\}

\displaystyle\text{and}

(3.15)

\displaystyle T_{L}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}:\begin{cases}\ell_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\leq t_{j}\leq\ell_{j}+\frac{1}{2}&\text{if $j\in D_{L}$}\\
0\leq t_{j}\leq\ell_{j}+\frac{1}{2}&\text{if $j\notin D_{L}$}\end{cases}\Bigg\}=\bigcup\nolimits_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{L}}Q_{L^{\prime}}

Thus if
D_{L}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
or
D_{L}=\emptyset
then
T_{L}=Q_{L}
is a cube and
\mathcal{T}_{L}=L
. If
\emptyset\neq D\subsetneq\{1,\ldots,n\}
then
T_{L}
is a tube of side length
1
in the directions of
\mathbf{e}_{j}
if
j\in D_{L}
and length
\ell_{j}+\frac{1}{2}
in the direction of
\mathbf{e}_{j}
if
j\notin D_{L}
. The following lemma says that the sets
\{\mathcal{T}_{L}:L\in\mathcal{L}\}
are a partition of
\mathbb{N}^{n}
and the tubes
\{T_{L}:L\in\mathcal{L}\}
cover
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
with disjoint interiors.

Lemma 3.7
.

(a)

If
Q_{M}\in\mathcal{Q}_{D}
there is a unique maximal element
Q_{L}\in\mathcal{Q}_{D}
with
Q_{M}\subseteq T_{L}
.

(b)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}=\bigcup\nolimits_{D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}}\Big(\bigcup\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}(D)}T_{L}\Big)
.

(c)

If
L_{1}\neq L_{2}
are in
\mathcal{L}
, then
T_{L_{1}}\cap T_{L_{2}}
is closed with measure zero.

Proof.

Let
D=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}\}
. If
M=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
and
Q_{M}\in\mathcal{Q}_{D}
then
M\in\Gamma_{S}
for a marked partition
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
and from (
3.8
) we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq m_{j}\leq\frac{1}{e(k_{r},j)}m_{k_{r}}

\displaystyle\text{for all $j\in A_{r}$, \quad and \quad}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<m_{k_{p}}\leq\tau_{S}(k_{p},k_{q})m_{k_{q}}

\displaystyle\text{for all $1\leq p,q\leq s$.}

Put

\ell_{j}=\begin{cases}m_{j}&\text{if $j=k_{r}\in D$}\\
\lfloor e(k_{r},j)^{-1}m_{k_{r}}\rfloor&\text{if $j\in A_{r}$ and $j\neq k_{r}$}\end{cases}
.
Then
L
satisfies the inequalities of (
3.13
) and so
Q_{L}\in\mathcal{Q}_{D}
and
Q_{L}
is maximal. Since
m_{j}\leq\ell_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
it follows that
M\in\mathcal{T}_{L}
and so
Q_{M}\in T_{L}
. Since cube maximal in
\mathcal{L}(D)
are determined by the dotted entries, this proves (
a
).
If
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
then
\mathbf{t}\in Q_{M}
for some
M\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, and so (
b
) follows from (
a
). Finally if two distinct sets
\mathcal{T}_{L}
intersect in a set of positive measure then the intersection contains a cube
Q_{L}
, and so (
c
) follows from (
a
).
∎

Although the sets
\mathcal{L}(D)
partition
\mathcal{L}
, we will require a finer partition indexed by marked partitions
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}\cup\{\emptyset\}
rather than by sets of dotted entries. This partition involves a certain, though irrelevant, amount of arbitrariness. We choose pairwise disjoint subsets
\mathcal{L}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(D)
such that
\mathcal{L}_{\emptyset}=\{0\}
,
\mathcal{L}_{S}
contains all
L\in\mathcal{L}\cap\text{\rm Int\,}\Gamma(S)
, and
\mathcal{L}(D)=\bigcup\nolimits_{D(S)=D}\mathcal{L}_{S}
. Thus if
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
it follows from (
3.13
) that
\ell_{i}=\left\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\right\rfloor
for all
i\in A_{r}
and
\ell_{k_{p}}<\tau_{S}(k_{p},k_{q})\ell_{k_{q}}
for all
1\leq p\neq q\leq s
. In addition to the decomposition in Lemma
3.7
(
b
) we also have the alternative decomposition

\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}=\bigcup\nolimits_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}}\Big(\bigcup\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}T_{L}\Big).

(3.16)

This now leads to decompositions of the frequency space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. With
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
defined in (
3.2
) and setting
B_{L}=\pi^{-1}(T_{L})
for
L\in\mathcal{L}
, we have the decompositions
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\bigcup\nolimits_{D\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}}\big(\bigcup\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}(D)}B_{L}\big)
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\bigcup\nolimits_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}}\big(\bigcup\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}B_{L}\big)
.
The sets
B_{L}
are the basic dyadic blocks in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for the multi-norm structure generated by
\mathbf{E}
.
We prove that the sets
B_{L}
are comparable to products of dyadic balls and annuli in coordinate subspaces and
N_{i}
-norms depending on the marked partition
S
such that
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
with
S=\big\{(A_{r},k_{r}):r=1,\dots,s\big\}
. Then there are constants
c,C>0
depending only on the matrix
\mathbf{E}
such that

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{r=1}^{s}\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}:N_{k_{r}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}})\leq c2^{\ell_{k_{r}}}\,,\,|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}|>(c/2)2^{\ell_{k_{r}}}\big\}

(3.17)

\displaystyle\subset B_{L}\subset\prod\nolimits_{r=1}^{s}\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}:N_{k_{r}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}})\leq C2^{\ell_{k_{r}}}\,,\,|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}|>(C/2)2^{\ell_{k_{r}}}\big\}\ .

Proof.

By (
3.15
),

\displaystyle B_{L}=\pi^{-1}(T_{L})

\displaystyle=\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}2^{\ell_{j}}\leq|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}|\leq\sqrt{2}\,2^{\ell_{j}}\text{ if }j\in D_{L}\text{ and }|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}|\leq\sqrt{2}\,2^{\ell_{j}}\text{ if }j\not\in D_{L}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{r=1}^{s}\Big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}}:\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}2^{\ell_{k_{r}}}\leq|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}|\leq\sqrt{2}\,2^{\ell_{k_{r}}}\ ,\ |{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}|\leq\sqrt{2}\,2^{\ell_{j}}\text{ if }j\neq k_{r}\Big\}.

Given
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in B_{L}
and
r\in\{1,\dots,s\}
, we have
N_{k_{r}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}})=\max_{i\in A_{r}}|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|^{e(k_{r},i)}\leq\max_{i\in A_{r}}2^{e(k_{r},i)\ell_{i}}
. Since
\ell_{i}=\big\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\big\rfloor
,
N_{k_{r}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}})\leq 2^{\ell_{k_{r}}}
. The other inequality is obvious and this proves the second inclusion. The first inclusion is proved similarly.
∎

3.4.
Distances between tubes and
\Gamma(\mathbf{E})

We use the
\ell^{\infty}
-distance
|\mathbf{t}-\mathbf{u}|=\max_{i=1\dots,n}|t_{i}-u_{i}|
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

There is a constant
\kappa>0
depending only on
{\mathbf{E}}
such that

(i)

for every
L\in\mathcal{L}
, the distance from
L
to
\Gamma({\mathbf{E}})
is smaller than
\kappa
; more precisely, if
L\in\Gamma_{S}
, then the distance from
L
to
F_{S}
is smaller than
\kappa
;

(ii)

for every point
\mathbf{t}\in\Gamma({\mathbf{E}})
, the distance from
\mathbf{t}
to
\mathcal{L}
is smaller than
\kappa
;

(iii)

given
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
, the distance from
\bar{L}=L\wedge L^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
to
\mathcal{L}
is smaller than
\kappa
;

(iv)

if
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
and
T_{L}\cap T_{L^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
, then
|L-L^{\prime}|\leq\kappa
.

Proof.

For
\delta>0
consider the set

\Gamma_{\delta}=\Big\{\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}:\frac{t_{i}}{e(i,j)}\leq t_{j}+\delta\ ,\ \text{ for all }i,j\Big\}.

We show that points in
\Gamma_{\delta}
have uniformly bounded distances from
\Gamma({\mathbf{E}})
.
Clearly
\Gamma({\mathbf{E}})\subset\Gamma_{\delta}
. Let
\mathbf{t}\in\Gamma_{\delta}\setminus\Gamma({\mathbf{E}})
and fix a marked partition
S=\big\{(A_{r},k_{r}):r=1,\dots,s\big\}
such that
\mathbf{t}\in\Gamma_{S}
. Consider the point
\mathbf{u}\in F_{S}
with entries

u_{i}=t_{k_{r}}/e(k_{r},i)
if
i\in A_{r}
. By (
3.8
),
|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{t}|<\delta
. It suffices to prove that, for some
\delta>0
, every
L\in\mathcal{L}
is in
\Gamma_{\delta}
. This is obvious if
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S_{\rm p}}
. If
S
is non-principal, we use (
3.13
) to obtain an upper bound for the differences
d_{i,j}=\ell_{i}/e(i,j)-\ell_{j}
.
For
i\in A_{p}
and
j\in A_{q}
we have

d_{i,j}\leq\frac{\ell_{k_{p}}}{e(k_{p},i)e(i,j)}-\big(\frac{\ell_{k_{q}}}{e(k_{q},j)}-1\big)\leq\big(\frac{e(k_{p},k_{q})}{e(k_{p},i)e(i,j)}-\frac{1}{e(k_{q},j)}\big)\ell_{k_{q}}+1\leq\frac{1}{e(i,j)e(k_{q},j)}\big(\frac{e(k_{p},j)}{e(k_{p},i)}-e(i,j)\big)\ell_{k_{q}}+1\leq 1\ .

It follows that
\mathcal{L}\subset\Gamma_{\delta}
with
\delta=\max_{i,j}e(i,j)
, and this proves (
i
).
To prove (ii), let
L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{t}\in Q_{L}
. If
L\in\mathcal{L}
, we are finished. If
L\not\in L
, then there is
L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
such that
Q_{M}\subset T_{L}
, as defined in (
3.15
). The same formula easily implies that
L=L^{\prime}-\sum_{i\not\in D_{L^{\prime}}}p_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
with
p_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then (ii) will be proved if we show that, since
Q_{L}
intersects
\Gamma({\mathbf{E}})
, then
\max_{i\not\in D_{L^{\prime}}}p_{i}=|L-L^{\prime}|
must have an upper bound independent of
\mathbf{t}
. Write
\mathbf{t}
as
\mathbf{t}=L+\mathbf{u}=L^{\prime}-\sum_{i\not\in D_{L^{\prime}}}p_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{u}
, with
|u_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
for every
i
. Since
\mathbf{t}\in\Gamma({\mathbf{E}})
, setting
p_{i}=0
for
i\in D_{L^{\prime}}
, the conditions

\ell^{\prime}_{i}-p_{i}+u_{i}\leq e(i,j)(\ell^{\prime}_{j}-p_{j}+u_{j})

are satisfied for every
i,j
. If
L^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{S}
with
S=\big\{(A_{r},k_{r}):r=1,\dots,s\big\}
, we take
i=k_{r}
,
j\in A_{r}\setminus\{k_{r}\}
. Then (
3.13
) gives

\ell^{\prime}_{k_{r}}+u_{k_{r}}\leq e(k_{r},j)(\ell^{\prime}_{j}-p_{j}+u_{j})
,
so that

p_{j}\leq\ell^{\prime}_{j}-\frac{\ell^{\prime}_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},j)}+u_{j}-\frac{u_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},j)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big(1+\frac{1}{e(k_{r},j)}\Big),

and (
ii
) follows easily.
To prove (
iii
), observe that
\Gamma_{\delta}
is closed under
\wedge
. Then, for
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
, also
\bar{L}\in\Gamma_{\delta}
and the proof of (
i
) shows that
d\big(\bar{L},\Gamma({\mathbf{E}})\big)\leq C\delta
and the conclusion follows easily. To prove (
iv
), consider
L\in\Gamma_{S}
and
L^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{S^{\prime}}
such that there exists
{\mathbf{t}}\in T_{L}\cap T_{L^{\prime}}
. We let
S=\big\{(A_{r},k_{r}):r=1,\dots,s\big\}
,
S^{\prime}=\big\{(A^{\prime}_{r},k^{\prime}_{r}):r=1,\dots,s^{\prime}\big\}
., and
D=D_{S}
,
D^{\prime}=D_{S^{\prime}}
. There exist cubes
Q_{L-P}\subset T_{L}
and
Q_{L^{\prime}-P^{\prime}}\subset T_{L^{\prime}}
, with

P=\sum_{i\not\in D}p_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
,
P^{\prime}=\sum_{i\not\in D^{\prime}}p^{\prime}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
in
\mathbb{N}^{n}
such that
{\mathbf{t}}\in Q_{L-P}\cap Q_{L^{\prime}-P^{\prime}}
. We may assume that
p_{i}=0
for
i\in D
and
p^{\prime}_{i}=0
for
i\in D^{\prime}
. We remark that, since
|L-L^{\prime}|\leq|L-{\mathbf{t}}|+|{\mathbf{t}}-L^{\prime}|\leq|P|+|P^{\prime}|+n
, we must find uniform upper bounds for
p_{i}
and
p^{\prime}_{i}
. This is obvious for
i\in D\cap D^{\prime}
. Assume now that
i\in D\setminus D^{\prime}
. Since
p_{i}=0
and
p^{\prime}_{i}\geq 0
, we have
|t_{i}-\ell_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
, and
|t_{i}-\ell^{\prime}_{i}+p^{\prime}_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. In particular,
\ell_{i}\leq\ell^{\prime}_{i}+1
. Assume that
i\in A^{\prime}_{r}
and
k^{\prime}_{r}\in A_{q}
. Then, using (
3.13
),
p^{\prime}_{i}\leq\ell^{\prime}_{i}-t_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\leq\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}+1\leq\frac{\ell^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}_{r}}}{e(k^{\prime}_{r},i)}-\frac{\ell_{k_{q}}}{e(k_{q},i)}\leq\frac{\ell^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}_{r}}}{e(k^{\prime}_{r},i)}-\frac{e(k_{q},k^{\prime}_{r})}{e(k_{q},i)}\ell_{k^{\prime}_{r}}\leq\frac{\ell^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}_{r}}-\ell_{k^{\prime}_{r}}}{e(k^{\prime}_{r},i)}
. Since
k^{\prime}_{r}\in D^{\prime}
, it follows from the previous remark that
\ell^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}_{r}}\leq\ell_{k^{\prime}_{r}}+1
, so that
p^{\prime}_{i}\leq 1/e(k^{\prime}_{r},i)
. If
i\in D^{\prime}\setminus D
the argument is similar. It remains to consider the case
i\not\in D\cup D^{\prime}
.
We first observe that, if both
p_{i}
and
p^{\prime}_{i}
are strictly positive, say
1\leq p_{i}\leq p^{\prime}_{i}
, then also the translated cubes
Q_{L-P+p_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}
and
Q_{L^{\prime}-P^{\prime}+p_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}
have nonempty intersection and are contained in
T_{L}
,
T_{L^{\prime}}
respectively. We may then assume that
p_{i}=0
. Then the previous proof can be repeated.
∎

3.5.
Multi-norm multipliers and kernels

The paper
[
29
]
studies algebras
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathbf{E})
of singular integral operators associated with a standard matrix
\mathbf{E}
. The sizes of the Schwartz kernels of operators in
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathbf{E})
are controlled by the norms
N_{i}
and the corresponding multipliers in
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\mathbf{E})
are controlled by the norms
\widehat{N}_{i}
defined in (
2.5
). To give the precise definitions we need the following notation.

\circ

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the space of Schwartz functions and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the space of tempered distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then the quantities

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{(M)}=\sup\big\{\big|\mathbf{x}^{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}}\partial^{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\varphi(\mathbf{x})\big|:\text{$|{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}|+|\boldsymbol{\beta}|\leq M$ and $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$}\big\}

(3.18)

are the basic semi-norms defining the topology on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

\circ

The families of dilations
\delta_{t}^{i}
and
\widehat{\delta}_{t}^{i}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
were defined in (
2.5
). The homogeneous dimensions of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are given by

\displaystyle Q_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}e(i,j)^{-1}

\displaystyle\text{for the dilations $\delta_{t}^{i}$, and}

(3.19)

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}e(j,i)

\displaystyle\text{for the dilations $\widehat{\delta}_{t}^{i}$}.

For
{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, set
{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}^{j}=(\alpha_{k})_{k\in E_{j}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d_{j}}
, so that
{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}=({\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}^{1},\dots,{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}^{n})
. Then

\displaystyle[\![{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}^{j}]\!]

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{k\in E_{j}}\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}

\displaystyle\text{is the homogeneous length of ${\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}^{j}$ relative to the dilations \eqref{1.1},}

(3.20)

\displaystyle[\![{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}]\!]_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}e(j,i)[\![{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}^{j}]\!]

\displaystyle\text{is the homogeneous length of $\alpha$ relative to }\widehat{\delta}^{i}_{r}.

\circ

Given
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\omega\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{G})
, denote by
\mathcal{K}_{\omega}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{G^{c}})
the distribution such that

\langle\mathcal{K}_{\omega},\varphi\rangle=\langle\mathcal{K},\omega\otimes\varphi\rangle
for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{G^{c}})
. With an abuse of language we write
\mathcal{K}_{\omega}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{G}}\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}_{G},\cdot)\omega(\mathbf{x}_{G})\,d\mathbf{x}_{G}
.

\circ

If
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
, the Fourier transform is denoted by
\mathcal{F}[f]
or
\widehat{f}
.

The spaces of multi-norm kernels and multipliers are defined as follows. The symbol
\|\ \|
will be used to denote any (homogeneous) norm on the appropriate space, whose choice is irrelevant.

Definition 3.10
.

A.

The space of multi-norm kernels
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathbf{E})
is the set
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying the following conditions.

i)

On
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}

\mathcal{K}
is given by integration against a function
K\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\})
, and
for
{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
there is a constant
C_{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha},N}
so that for all
\mathbf{x}\neq{\mathbf{0}}

\big|\partial^{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}K(\mathbf{x})\big|\leq C_{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha},N}\Big(\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}(\mathbf{x})^{-q_{i}-[\![{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}_{i}]\!]}\Big)\big(1+\|\mathbf{x}\|\big)^{-N}\ .

(3.21)

ii)

For every
F\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
,
R\in\mathbb{R}^{F}_{+}
, and
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{F})
compactly supported on the unit ball
B_{F}(1)
with
\|\eta\|_{\mathcal{C}^{n}}\leq 1
, the distribution
\mathcal{K}_{R,\eta}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{F}}\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}_{F},\cdot)\eta(R\mathbf{x}_{F})\,d\mathbf{x}_{F}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{F^{c}})
is given on
\mathbb{R}^{F^{c}}\setminus\{0\}
by a smooth function
K_{R,\eta}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{F^{c}})
satisfying inequalities analogous to (
3.21
); precisely,

\displaystyle\big|\partial^{\alpha}K_{R,\eta}(\mathbf{x}_{F^{c}})\big|\leq C^{\prime}_{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha},N}\Big(\prod\nolimits_{i\in F^{c}}N_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{F^{c}})^{-q_{i}-[\![{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}_{i}]\!]}\Big)\big(1+\|\mathbf{x}_{F^{c}}\|\big)^{-N}

(3.22)

for every
\alpha,N
, with constants
C^{\prime}_{\alpha,N}
independent of
R,\eta
.

B.

The space of multi-norm multipliers
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\mathbf{E})
is the set of
m\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that every
{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
there is a constant
C_{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}}
so that

\displaystyle\big|\partial^{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}}m({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\big|\leq C_{\mathbf{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}}}\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\big(1+\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\big)^{-[\![{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}^{i}]\!]}.

(3.23)

We refer to the constants
C_{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha},N}
in (
3.21
) and
C^{\prime}_{\alpha,N}
in (
3.22
) as the
kernel constants
of
\mathcal{K}
and to the constants
C_{{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}}
in (
3.23
) as the
multiplier constants
of
m
.

Theorem 3.11
.

If
\mathbf{E}
is a standard matrix and
m\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the following properties are equivalent.

(1)

m\in\mathcal{M}_{\infty}(\mathbf{E})
.

(2)

m=\mathcal{F}\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

(3)

There exists
\big\{m_{L}:L\in\mathcal{L}\big\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm, so that

m({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}m_{L}(2^{-L}{\boldsymbol{\xi}})
and, if
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
and
D_{S}=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}\}
, then
m_{L}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=0
on the subspace
V_{k_{r}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{r}}={\mathbf{0}}\}
for
1\leq r\leq s
.

(4)

There exist
\big\{\eta_{L}:L\in\mathcal{L}\big\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm, so that
\mathcal{K}=\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\eta_{L}^{(-L)}

8
8

8

The notation
f^{(-L)}
is defined in (
2.9
).
with the sum converging in the sense of distributions and, if
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
with
S=\big\{(A_{r},k_{r})\,,\,r=1,\dots,s\big\}
, then
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k_{r}}}\eta_{L}(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}_{k_{r}}=0
for every
r=1,\dots,s
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially contained in
[
29
]
, though the statements are formulated with the following weaker conditions:

(
3^{\prime}
)

: same as (3) but with
m_{L}
vanishing on the smaller subspaces
V_{A_{r}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{A_{r}}={\mathbf{0}}\}
;

(
4^{\prime}
)

: same as (4) but with
\eta_{L}
having integral 0 in each block of coordinates
\mathbf{x}_{A_{r}}
.

The equivalence of (1), (2), (3’), (4’) is a summary of Theorems 4.1, 4.2, 6.13, 6.14 in
[
29
]
.
However, we will see in Section
4
below that
m({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}m({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\widehat{\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
where
\Psi_{L}
is defined in (
4.4
). If
L\in\mathcal{L}(D)
and
k\in D(S)
then
\Psi_{L}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has cancellation of order
m
in each set of variables
\mathbf{x}_{k}
. This gives the implications (1)
\Rightarrow
(3) and (2)
\Rightarrow
(4).
The implications
(3)\Rightarrow(3^{\prime})
and
(4)\Rightarrow(4^{\prime})
are obvious. Thus the equivalence of all six conditions is established.
∎

Convolutions of local smooth Calderón-Zygmund kernels adapted to different dilations are contained in the classes
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathbf{E})
for an appropriate matrix
{\mathbf{E}}
. Thus for
1\leq i\leq n
, let
m_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a local smooth
Mihlin-Hörmander multiplier
for the family of dilations
\{\widehat{\delta}_{r}^{i}:r>0\}
. This means that
\big|\partial^{\alpha}m_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\big|\leq C_{\alpha}\big(1+\widehat{N}_{i}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\big)^{-[\![{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}]\!]_{i}}
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Each
m_{i}
is the Fourier transforms of a local smooth
Calderón-Zygmund kernel

\mathcal{K}_{i}
;
i.e.

\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a distribution which, away from the origin, is given by a smooth function
K_{i}
satisfying

\big|\partial^{\alpha\!\!\!\!\alpha}K_{i}(\mathbf{x})\big|\leq C_{\alpha,N}\widehat{N}_{i}(\mathbf{x})^{-Q_{i}-[\![\alpha]\!]_{i}}\big(1+\|\mathbf{x}\|\big)^{-N}

together with cancellations in the full set of variables, i.e.,

\big|\langle\mathcal{K}_{i},\eta\circ\widehat{\delta}^{i}_{r}\rangle\big|\leq C
,
for all
r>0
and
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
supported on the unit cube
B
and with
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\leq 1
. Then the following follows from
[
29
, Theorem 10.2]
.

Theorem 3.12
.

Suppose that
{\mathbf{E}}
is a standard matrix. For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\mathcal{K}_{i}
be a local smooth Calderón-Zygmund kernel relative to the dilations
\widehat{\delta}^{i}_{r}
given in (
2.5
) and let
m_{i}=\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
be the corresponding local multiplier. Then
m=\prod_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}
is in
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}({\mathbf{E}})
and the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{1}*\cdots*\mathcal{K}_{n}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Remarks 3.13
.

1.

The strict inequality
1<e(j,k)e(k,j)
, which is the third requirement in (
2.4
) for a standard matrix, is not required either in
[
29
, Theorem 10.2]
or in Theorem
3.12
, but as explained in
[
29
, Section 14.4]
, if equality occurs for some
j\neq k
, then the two families of dilations
\{\delta_{r}^{j}\},\{\delta_{r}^{k}\}
coincide, up to a re-parametrization, and the same holds for the dilations
\{\widehat{\delta}_{r}^{j}\}
and
\{\widehat{\delta}_{r}^{k}\}
. Consequently the norms
N_{j}
and
N_{k}
, as well as
\widehat{N}_{j}
and
\widehat{N}_{k}
, are paired by the property of being one a power of the other. At the same time, the two quantities
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}|
,
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k}|
appear with proportional exponents in each norm
\widehat{N}_{i}
, so that the product
\mathbb{R}^{d_{j}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}}
can be regarded as a single factor, reducing by one the dimension of the matrix
{\mathbf{E}}
. Hence the strict inequality is a non-reducibility condition and it will be assumed throughout this paper.

2.

If
{\mathbf{E}}
is standard it can be proved that
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}({\mathbf{E}})
is minimal in the following sense: it is the smallest class of smooth multipliers that is closed under pointwise multiplication and contains the local smooth Mihlin-Hörmander multipliers for all dilations
\{\widehat{\delta}^{i}_{r}\}
,
i=1,\dots,n
.
Theorem 10.2 in
[
29
]
also proves that, given any number of dilations
\widetilde{\delta}_{r}^{\nu}
with exponents
\lambda(1,\nu),\dots,\lambda(n,\nu)
, and corresponding smooth local Mihlin-Hörmander multipliers
m_{\nu}
, then the product
m=\prod_{\nu}m_{\nu}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}({\mathbf{E}})
where
{\mathbf{E}}
has entries
e(i,j)=\max_{\nu}\frac{\lambda(i,\nu)}{\lambda(j,\nu)}
.
This matrix
{\mathbf{E}}
satisfies the first two conditions in (
2.4
) but is not necessarily standard. It may also happen that
{\mathbf{E}}
is standard but
\mathcal{M}_{\infty}({\mathbf{E}})
does not satisfy the minimality condition relative to the dilations
\widetilde{\delta}_{r}^{\nu}
defined above. See
[
29
, Section 10.3]
.

3.6.
The cases
n=2
and
n=3

The dyadic decomposition
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}=\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}}T_{L}
and the corresponding Littlewood-Paley decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{d}=B(1)\cup\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\pi^{-1}(T_{L})
are hard to visualize, and it may help to see what they mean when
n
is small.

3.6.1.
Case
n=2

Consider
\mathbb{R}^{2}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
and the standard matrix
\mathbf{E}={\left[\begin{matrix}1&a\\
b&1\end{matrix}\right]}
with
ab>1
.
9
9

9

With 2 factors any pair of distinct dilations can be reduced to the present form. This is no longer the case with
n\geq 3
factors. The assumption
d_{1}=d_{2}=1
is just for notational simplicity and is not essential at all.
In this case
\Gamma(\mathbf{E})=\big\{(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:t_{1}/a\leq t_{2}\leq bt_{1}\big\}
with dilations
\widehat{\delta}^{1}_{r}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=(r\xi_{1},r^{b}\xi_{2})
and
\widehat{\delta}^{2}_{r}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=(r^{a}\xi_{1},r\xi_{2})
. The relative homogeneous norms are
\widehat{N}_{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\max\big\{|\xi_{1}|,|\xi_{2}|^{\frac{1}{b}}\big\}
and
\widehat{N}_{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\max\big\{|\xi_{1}|^{1/a},|\xi_{2}|\big\}
.

There are three marked partitions of
\{1,2\}
:
S_{p}=\{\overset{.}{1}\}\{\overset{.}{2}\}
(the principal partition),
S_{1}=\{\overset{.}{1},2\}
,
S_{2}=\{1,\overset{.}{2}\}
. The frequency space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the union of four essentially disjoint regions,
\mathbb{R}^{2}=B(1)\cup\widehat{E}_{S_{1}}\cup\widehat{E}_{1}\cup\widehat{E}_{2}
where
B(1)=\big\{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):|\xi_{1}|\leq 1,\,|\xi_{2}|\leq 1\big\}
and the other sets are as follows.

1.

\widehat{E}_{p}
is the set where
1\leq|\xi_{1}|
is dominant in
\widehat{N}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
and
1\leq|\xi_{2}|
is dominant in
\widehat{N}_{2}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
,
i.e.
where
|\xi_{1}|^{1/a}\leq|\xi_{2}|\leq|\xi_{1}|^{b}
. Here
\widehat{N}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=|\xi_{1}|
and
\widehat{N}_{2}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=|\xi_{2}|
. This is the “product” region.

2.

\widehat{E}_{1}
is the set where
1\leq|\xi_{1}|
is dominant in both
\widehat{N}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
and
\widehat{N}_{2}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
;
i.e.
