, the functions, uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm, such that

\displaystyle\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}=\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}\ ,\qquad\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i,\ell^{\prime}_{i}}^{(-\ell^{\prime}_{i})_{i}}=\sigma^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}\ ,\qquad\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\sigma}_{i,\ell^{\prime}_{i}}^{(-\ell^{\prime}_{i})_{i}}=\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}\ .

(6.4)

Using
\delta_{\mathbf{0}}=\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\Big(\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\Big)*\Big(\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\sigma}_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\Big)

write
f*\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}=\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\big(f*\sigma^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}\big)*\big(\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}*\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}\big)
. We need the following analogue of Lemma
5.3
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
L^{\#}=(L\wedge L^{\prime})\,\widetilde{}=\widetilde{L}\wedge\widetilde{L^{\prime}}=(\ell^{\#}_{1},\dots,\ell^{\#}_{n})
. Then
L^{\#}\in\mathcal{L}
and, given
M>0
, there is
\varepsilon>0
such that the inequality
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\big(1+2^{\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}}|x_{i}|\big)\Big)^{M}\big|\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}*\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}(x)\big|\,dx\leq C_{M}2^{-\varepsilon|L-L^{\prime}|}
holds uniformly in
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, provided the order
m
of cancellations of the
\widetilde{\sigma}_{i,\ell^{\prime}_{i}}
is large enough relative to
M
.

Proof.

The equality
(L\wedge L^{\prime})\,\widetilde{}=\widetilde{L}\wedge\widetilde{L^{\prime}}
is obvious.
Let
\bar{L}=L\wedge L^{\prime}=(\bar{\ell}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\ell}_{n})
. Then for each
i
,

\eta_{i,L}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}*\widetilde{\sigma}_{i,\ell^{\prime}_{i}}^{(-\ell^{\prime}_{i})_{i}}=2^{-\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|}\zeta_{i,L,L^{\prime}}^{(-\bar{\ell}_{i})_{i}}
,
with
\zeta_{i,L,L^{\prime}}
uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm with respect to
i,L,L^{\prime}
. According to Lemma
5.2
,

\gamma_{i}=\begin{cases}\bar{\lambda}_{i}m&\text{ if }\ell^{\prime}_{i}>\ell_{i}\\
\bar{\lambda}_{i}&\text{ if }\ell^{\prime}_{i}\leq\ell_{i}\ .\end{cases}

Also from Lemma
4.5
(i),

\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}*\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}=2^{-\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|}\zeta^{*}_{L,L^{\prime}}{}^{(-L^{\#})}
, with the
\zeta^{*}_{L,L^{\prime}}
uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm.
For any
M
, we then have
\big|\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}*\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}(x)\big|\leq C_{M}2^{\sum_{i}q_{i}\ell_{i}^{\#}-\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\big(1+2^{\ell_{i}^{\#}}|x_{i}|\big)^{-2M}
, and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\big(1+2^{\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}}|x_{i}|\big)\Big)^{M}

\displaystyle\big|\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}*\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}(x)\big|\,dx

\displaystyle\leq C_{M}2^{\sum_{i}q_{i}\ell_{i}^{\#}-\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}}\frac{\big(1+2^{\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}}|x_{i}|\big)^{M}}{\big(1+2^{\ell_{i}^{\#}}|x_{i}|\big)^{2M}}\,dx_{i}

\displaystyle=C_{M}2^{-\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}}\frac{\big(1+2^{\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}^{\#}}|x_{i}|\big)^{M}}{\big(1+|x_{i}|\big)^{2M}}\,dx_{i}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}_{M}2^{\sum_{i}\big(M(\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}^{\#})-\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|\big)}.

It suffices to prove that, for some
\varepsilon>0
and
C^{\prime\prime}_{M}
independent of
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
we have

\sum\nolimits_{i}\big(M(\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}^{\#})-\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|\big)\leq C^{\prime\prime}_{M}-\varepsilon\sum\nolimits_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|.

(6.5)

If
\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}\leq\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
, the
i
-th term in the sum gives the inequality
M(\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}^{\#})-\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|=-\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|
. In order to estimate the terms for which
\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}>\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
, i.e., for which
\ell_{i}^{\#}=\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
, it is necessary to group terms appropriately.
10
10

10

The following argument is the same used in
[
29
]
Section 3.1 to obtain the “dominance principle” allowing to associate a marked partition to each point in the unit ball in the
x
-space.

Let
\mathbf{t}_{L}\in\Gamma(\mathbf{E})
have entries
t_{L,i}=\min_{j=1,\dots,n}e(i,j)\ell_{j}
. Suppose that, for some
i
,
t_{L,i}=e(i,k)\ell_{k}<\ell_{i}
(with
k\neq i
).
Using the basic assumptions, it is easy to verify that
t_{L,k}=\ell_{k}
.
If we select the indices
k_{1},\dots,k_{s}
for which
t_{L,k_{r}}=\ell_{k_{r}}
, we can then partition
\{1,\dots,n\}
into subsets
A_{r}
with the property that
t_{L,i}=e(i,k_{r})\ell_{k_{r}}
for all
i\in A_{r}
(this partition may not be unique if some equality
e(i,j)\ell_{j}=e(i,j^{\prime})\ell_{j^{\prime}}
occurs).
Let
A^{\prime}_{r}
be the set of
i\in A_{r}
such that
\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}>\widetilde{\ell}_{i}
, and

S^{\prime}_{r}=\sum_{i\in A^{\prime}_{r}}\big(M(\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}-\widetilde{\ell}_{i})-\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|\big)\ .

For
i\in A^{\prime}_{r}
we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\ell}^{\prime}_{i}-\widetilde{\ell}_{i}\leq t_{L^{\prime},i}-t_{L,i}+1\leq e(i,k_{r})\big(t_{L^{\prime},k_{r}}-\ell_{k_{r}}\big)+1+\frac{e(i,k_{r})}{2}\leq e(i,k_{r})(\ell^{\prime}_{k_{r}}-\ell_{k_{r}})+C\ ,

with
C
depending only on
\mathbf{E}
. Therefore

S^{\prime}_{r}\leq\sum_{i\in A^{\prime}_{r}}\big(Me(i,k_{r})(\ell^{\prime}_{k_{r}}-\ell_{k_{r}})+MC-\gamma_{i}|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|\big).

The terms
Me(i,k_{r})(\ell^{\prime}_{k_{r}}-\ell_{k_{r}})
can be neglected if
\ell^{\prime}_{k_{r}}\leq\ell_{k_{r}}
.
If
\ell^{\prime}_{k_{r}}>\ell_{k_{r}}
, then the constant
\gamma_{k_{r}}
can be made
>M\sum_{i\in A^{\prime}_{r}}\big(e(i,k_{r})
by choosing
m
large enough. Summing over
r
, we obtain (
6.5
) as required, and this proves Lemma
6.8
.
∎

We now continue with the proof of Proposition
6.7
. In analogy with (
5.5
), we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{M,L}(x)=\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\big|f*\sigma^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}(x-x^{\prime})\big|}{\prod_{i}\big(1+2^{\widetilde{\ell}_{i}}|x^{\prime}_{i}|\big)^{M}}

for
M>0
,
L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, and
\sigma^{*}_{L}
as defined in (
6.4
),
We then have

\displaystyle\big|f*\eta_{L}^{*}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\!\!\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int\big|f*\sigma^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}(x-x^{\prime})\big|\big|\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}*\widetilde{\sigma}^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L^{\prime}})}(x^{\prime})\big|\,dx^{\prime}\leq\!\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}2^{-\varepsilon|L-L^{\prime}|}\widetilde{\omega}_{M,L^{\prime}}(x)

and so
\Big(\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\big|f*\eta^{*}_{L}{}^{(-\widetilde{L})}(x)\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\Big(\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\Big(\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}2^{-\varepsilon|L-L^{\prime}|}\widetilde{\omega}_{M,L^{\prime}}\Big)^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\Big(\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\widetilde{\omega}_{M,L}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

by Young’s inequality on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. We need the following analogue of Lemma
5.6
with
(\Psi^{\#}_{L,L^{\prime}})^{(-L^{\prime})}
replaced by
\sigma^{**}_{L,L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L}^{\prime})}=\Big(\mathop{*}_{\{i:\ell^{\prime}_{i}>\ell_{i}\}}\sigma_{i,\ell^{\prime}_{i}}^{(-\ell^{\prime}_{i})_{i}}\Big)*\Big(\mathop{*}_{\{i:\ell^{\prime}_{i}=\ell_{i}\}}\rho_{i,\ell^{\prime}_{i}}^{(-\ell^{\prime}_{i})_{i}}\Big)
, and similarly for
(\widetilde{\Psi}^{\#}_{L,L^{\prime}})^{(-L^{\prime})}
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
M>0
. Then, for every positive
\mu
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{M,L}(x)\leq C_{M,\mu}\sum_{L^{\prime}\geq L}2^{-\mu|L-L^{\prime}|}\big|f*\sigma^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-\widetilde{L}^{\prime})}\big|*\chi_{M}^{(-\widetilde{L})}(x)\ .

We omit the proof, which follows the same lines and is actually simpler.
The proof of Proposition
6.7
now proceeds as in Theorem
5.1
with the obvious modifications.
∎

Theorem 6.10
.

Let
\{\sigma_{i,\ell}\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\,,\,\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family as in Section
4.3
,
S^{\prime}_{\Sigma}
as in (
4.8
) and
S^{\prime\prime}_{\sigma}
as in (
6.2
). Then, given
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the two conditions
S^{\prime}_{\Sigma}f\in L^{1}
and
S^{\prime\prime}_{\sigma}f\in L^{1}
are equivalent.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.7
, it suffices to prove the equivalence for an appropriately chosen family
\{\sigma_{i,\ell}\}
.
We then take
\sigma_{i,\ell}
with Fourier transform supported in
\big\{\xi:\widehat{N}_{i}(\xi)\leq 1+\delta\big\}
if
\ell=0
and in
\big\{\xi:\frac{1}{2}-\delta\leq\widehat{N}_{i}(\xi)\leq 1+\delta\big\}
if
\ell>0
, with
\delta>0
sufficiently small.
It follows from Lemma
3.9
(iv) that
\sigma^{*}_{L}=0
if
|L-L^{\prime}|>\kappa+1
for all
L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
and therefore, for
L\in\mathcal{L}_{D}
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{L}^{(-L)}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{*}_{i\in D}\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\Big)*\Big(\mathop{*}_{i\not\in D}\rho_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\Big)=\Big(\mathop{*}_{i\in D}\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\Big)*\Big(\mathop{*}_{i\not\in D}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}_{i}\leq\ell_{i}}\sigma_{i,\ell^{\prime}_{i}}^{(-\ell^{\prime}_{i})_{i}}\Big)=\sum_{|L^{\prime}-L|\leq A}\sigma^{*}_{L^{\prime}}{}^{(-L^{\prime})}.

Then the conclusion is a consequence of the following two remarks.

1.

The square function
S^{\prime\prime}_{\sigma}
can be regarded as the square function associated to a family
\{\gamma_{i,L}\}
as in Proposition
6.3
.

2.

the square function
S^{\prime}_{\Sigma}
can be regarded as a finite sum of square functions in the left-hand side of (
6.3
), each associated to a family
\{\eta_{i,L}\}
as in Proposition
6.7
. ∎

7.
Dyadic cubes and rectangles in
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\mathbf{x}}

and square functions of Plancherel-Pólya type

We shall need the definition of dyadic cubes and rectangles in our multi-norm context. Since several families of dilations are involved, the definition requires some care. We will only focus our attention on cubes and rectangles at scales smaller than unit scale.

7.1.
Dyadic cubes

The dilations
\delta_{t}
from (
1.1
) act on each factor of
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
; if
\mathbf{x}_{i}=(x_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}
then
\delta_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{i})=\big(t^{\lambda_{h}}x_{h}\big)_{h\in E_{i}}
.
We define dyadic cubes
\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
at scale
2^{-\ell_{i}}
for
\ell_{i}\geq 0
which respect this family of dilations. This is done by induction on
\ell_{i}
. For
\ell_{i}=0
the dyadic cubes at scale
2^{0}=1
are the unit cubes
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{0}=\big\{(x_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}:p_{h}\leq x_{h}<p_{h}+1\big\}
where
\mathbf{p}=(p_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{i}}
. We denote this family by
\mathbb{D}_{i}^{0}
. The cubes at scale
2^{-\ell_{i}}
should then have side lengths comparable to
\big\{2^{-\ell_{i}\lambda_{h}}:h\in E_{i}\big\}
and be comparable to translates of
\big\{(x_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}:0\leq x_{h}<2^{-\ell_{i}\lambda_{h}}\big\}
.
We cannot simply divide the sides of dyadic cubes by the same number at each stage unless the exponents
\lambda_{h}
are rational multiples of each other.

To deal with this problem, for
h\in E_{i}
we say that a sequence
\big\{\mu(\ell_{i},h)\in\mathbb{N},\ell_{i}\geq 0\big\}
is
admissible
if it is non-decreasing, if
\mu(0,h)=0
, and if there exists
a>0
so that

\displaystyle 2^{\ell_{i}\lambda_{h}-a}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\mu(\ell_{i},h)}\leq 2^{\ell_{i}\lambda_{h}+a},

\displaystyle\text{ or equivalently }

\displaystyle|\mu(\ell_{i},h)-\ell_{i}\lambda_{h}|

\displaystyle\leq a.

(7.1)

Given an admissible sequence, suppose that the family of cubes
\mathbb{D}_{i}^{\ell_{i}-1}=\big\{\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}-1}\big\}
has been defined so that the
h^{th}
side of
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}-1}
has length
2^{-\mu(\ell_{i}-1,h)}
. Then we divide the
h^{th}
-side of each cube into
2^{\mu(\ell_{i},h)-\mu(\ell_{i}-1,h)}
equal pieces. The result of this division is the family
\mathbb{D}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}=\big\{\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}:\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{i}}\big\}
where

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}=\Big\{(x_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}:2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}p_{h}\leq x_{h}<2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}(p_{h}+1)\Big\}.

(7.2)

The side lengths of these rectangular sets are not equal, but we call them
dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
because their scale depends on a single dyadic parameter
2^{-\ell_{i}}
compatible with the given dilations. The following properties of dyadic cubes are then clear.

(1)

\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}=\bigcup_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{i}}}{\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}}
is a disjoint union.

(2)

The
h^{th}
side of
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
has length
2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}
.

(3)

If
0\leq m\leq\ell_{i}
and if
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}\in\mathbb{D}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}
there is a unique dyadic cube
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}^{\ell_{i}-m}\in\mathbb{D}_{i}^{\ell_{i}-m}
at scale
2^{m-\ell_{i}}
containing
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
. We use the notation

\displaystyle 2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}=\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}^{\ell_{i}-m}.

(7.3)

It may happen that
2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
denotes the same set for more than one value of
m
since the coefficients
\mu(\ell_{i},h)
are not assumed to be strictly increasing in
\ell_{i}
.

(4)

Each dyadic cube at scale
2^{-\ell_{i}}
is a disjoint union of
\prod_{h\in E_{i}}2^{\mu(\ell_{i}+1,h)-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}
cubes in
\mathbb{D}_{i}^{\ell_{i}+1}
.

(5)

If
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}^{\ell_{i}^{\prime}}
are cubes with

\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}\cap\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}^{\ell_{i}^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
then

\begin{cases}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}=\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}^{\ell_{i}^{\prime}}&\text{if $\ell_{i}=\ell_{i}^{\prime}$}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}\subseteq\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}}^{\ell_{i}^{\prime}}&\text{if $\ell_{i}^{\prime}<\ell_{i}$}\end{cases}
.

Remarks 7.1
.

1.

In this definition of dyadic cubes, the precise choice of the admissible sequence
\mu(\ell_{i},h)
is not important. The most natural choice is to take
\mu(\ell_{i},h)=\lfloor\ell_{i}\lambda_{h}\rfloor
, with
\lfloor x\rfloor
denoting the largest integer less than or equal to
x\in\mathbb{R}
, but other choices are used, for example, in the proof of Proposition
7.2
.

2.

This construction of dyadic cubes can be done more generally in any vector space with a family of dilations. We do this in Section
7.3
below.

7.2.
Dyadic rectangles in the product
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}

Dyadic rectangles
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
are by definition the product of dyadic cubes in each factor. Thus if
L=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and
\mathbf{P}=(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{Z}^{d_{n}}
then
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{P}}^{L}=\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
is a typical dyadic rectangle at scale
2^{-L}
. We use the following notation.

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{L}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}:P\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\Big\},

\displaystyle\text{the set of all dyadic rectangles at scale $2^{-L}$;}

(7.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\nolimits_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\mathbb{D}^{L},

\displaystyle\text{the set of all dyadic rectangles at scales $\leq 1$};

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\mathbb{D}^{L},

\displaystyle\text{the set of all dyadic rectangles at scale $2^{-L}$ with $L\in\mathcal{L}$};

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{S}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}\mathbb{D}^{L},

\displaystyle\text{the set of all dyadic rectangles at scale $2^{-L}$ with $L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}$}.

Recall from the end of Section
2.1
that
\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{E}}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}\cup\{\emptyset\}
. Then
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{L}}=\bigcup\nolimits_{S\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathbf{E}}}\mathbb{D}_{S}
where
\mathbb{D}_{\emptyset}=\mathbb{D}^{0}
.

7.3.
Dyadic rectangles in the product
\mathbb{R}^{A_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{A_{s}}

Let
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
. If
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
and
\mathbf{R}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}
, we have the coarser decomposition
\mathbf{R}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}\mathbf{R}_{A_{r}}
where
\mathbf{R}_{A_{r}}=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
. We show that the
dyadic rectangle

\mathbf{R}_{A_{r}}
is actually the same as a
dyadic cube
relative to an appropriate family of dilations in the subspace
\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
. Recall from (
2.5
)
\delta_{t}^{i}(\mathbf{x})=\big(t^{\frac{1}{e(i,1)}}\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,t^{\frac{1}{e(i,n)}}\mathbf{x}_{n}\big)
. We introduce the
s
-parameter dilations
\delta_{\mathbf{t}}^{S}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by setting

\displaystyle\delta^{S}_{\mathbf{t}}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\big(\delta^{k_{1}}_{t_{1}}\mathbf{x}_{A_{1}},\dots,\delta^{k_{s}}_{t_{s}}\mathbf{x}_{A_{s}}\big)

\displaystyle\text{ where }

\displaystyle\delta_{t_{r}}^{k_{r}}(\mathbf{x}_{A_{r}})

\displaystyle=\big(t_{r}^{\frac{1}{e(k_{r},i)}}\mathbf{x}_{i}\big)_{i\in A_{r}}.

(7.5)

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
, let
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
and let
\mathbf{R}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}
, where each
\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}
is constructed according to (
7.2
) using the admissible sequences
\big\{\mu(\ell_{i},h)\in\mathbb{N},\ell_{i}\geq 0\big\}
for
i\in E_{i}
. Then for each
1\leq r\leq s
the dyadic rectangle
\mathbf{R}_{A_{r}}=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
is a dyadic cube in
\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
relative the to dilations
\delta_{t_{r}}^{k_{r}}
restricted to
\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
, constructed as in (
7.2
) using the admissible sequences
\Big\{\nu(\ell_{k_{r}},h)=\mu\Big(\Big\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\Big\rfloor,h\Big):\ell_{k_{r}}\geq 0\Big\}
for
h\in E_{i}
,
i\in A_{r}
.

Proof.

On the one hand

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{A_{r}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}\Big\{(x_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}:2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}p_{h}\leq x_{h}<2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}(p_{h}+1)\text{ for all }h\in E_{i}\Big\}.

Since
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
we have
\ell_{i}=\left\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\right\rfloor
for all
i\in A_{r}
and so

\mathbf{R}_{A_{r}}=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}\prod_{h\in E_{i}}\Big\{x_{h}:2^{-\mu\left(\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\rfloor,h\right)}p_{h}\leq x_{h}<2^{-\mu\left(\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\rfloor,h\right)}(p_{h}+1)\Big\}.

(7.6)

Thus scales on
\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
are indexed by the integer
\ell_{k_{r}}
.

On the other hand, as in Section
7.1
, we can use the family of dilations
\delta_{t}^{k_{r}}
to construct dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
.
If
\big\{\nu(\ell_{k_{r}},h):\ell_{k_{r}}\geq 0\big\}
is an admissible sequence for
h\in\bigcup_{i\in A_{r}}E_{i}
, then a dyadic cube in
\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
relative to these dilations has the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}^{\ell_{k_{r}}}=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}\prod_{h\in E_{i}}\big\{x_{h}:2^{-\nu(\ell_{k_{r}},h)}p_{h}\leq x_{h}<2^{-\nu(\ell_{k_{r}},h)}(p_{h}+1)\big\}\quad\text{where $\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{A_{r}}$}.

(7.7)

Comparing equations (
7.6
) and (
7.7
), we see that if
\nu(\ell_{k_{r}},h)=\mu\left(\left\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\right\rfloor,h\right)
then
\mathbf{R}_{A_{r}}=\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}^{\ell_{k_{r}}}
. It remains to show that with this definition,
\big\{\nu(\ell_{k_{r}},h):\ell_{k_{r}}\geq 0\big\}
is an admissible sequence. But

\displaystyle\Big|\nu(\ell_{k_{r}},h)-\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}\lambda_{h}}{e(k_{r},i)}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\mu_{\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\rfloor,h}\!-\!\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}\lambda_{h}}{e(k_{r},i)}\Big|\leq\Big|\mu_{\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\rfloor,h}\!-\!\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\rfloor\lambda_{h}\Big|+\Big|\lfloor\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}}{e(k_{r},i)}\rfloor\lambda_{h}-\frac{\ell_{k_{r}}\lambda_{h}}{e(k_{r},i)}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq a+\lambda_{h}\leq a+\sup\{\lambda_{h}:1\leq h\leq d\},

and this shows that
\{\nu(\ell_{k_{r}},h):\text{$h\in E_{i}$ and $i\in A_{r}$}\}
is an admissible sequence for
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{A}_{r}}
if
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
. This completes the proof.
∎

The last statement justifies the following alternative notation for
\mathbf{R}_{A_{r}}
.

Definition 7.3
.

For
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
,
\mathbf{R}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}\in\mathbb{D}_{L}
and
r=1,\dots,s
, we define
\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}^{\ell_{k_{r}}}=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\ell_{i}}
.

This allows us to write
\mathbf{R}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}^{\ell_{k_{r}}}
, a notation that only refers to the coarser decomposition

\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{A_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{A_{s}}
. The basic properties of dyadic cubes are satisfies by the
\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}^{\ell_{k_{r}}}
. In particular, two such cubes are either disjoint or one contained in the other.

7.4.
Enlargements of dyadic rectangles

In this section we refer to a fixed marked partition
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
, with the associated decomposition

\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{A_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{A_{s}}
and the dilations
\delta^{S}_{\mathbf{t}}
in (
7.5
). We will use two kinds of “enlargements” of dyadic rectangles
\mathbf{R}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}
. Though we will ultimately be only interested with rectangles of less that unit size, it is not relevant to impose such a restriction at this stage.

7.4.1.
Dyadic enlargements

If
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
is a dyadic cube and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the dyadic enlargement
2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
was defined in (
7.3
). If
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and
\mathbf{R}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
, then
2^{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{R}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}2^{m_{i}}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
. As in Section
7.3
, if
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
and if
\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
then
2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}=\prod_{i\in A_{r}}2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}}^{\ell_{i}}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. If
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{s})\in\mathbb{N}^{s}
then
2^{\mathbf{m}}\prod_{r=1}^{s}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}2^{m_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}
.
If
B\subset\{1,\dots,s\}
and
\mathbf{m}_{B}\in\mathbb{N}^{B}
, we use the notation
2^{\mathbf{m}_{B}}\mathbf{R}
, with a slight abuse of notation, to denote an enlargement only in the variables in
B
.
To denote enlargement in a single variable
\mathbf{x}_{r}
let
\mathbf{e}_{r}=(\dots,0,1,0,\dots)
be the unit vector with the 1 in
r
-th position, and write
2^{m\mathbf{e}_{r}}\mathbf{R}=2^{m_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}\times\Big(\prod\nolimits_{r^{\prime}\neq r}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r^{\prime}}}\Big)
.

Remarks 7.4
.

(1)

Denoting the homogeneous dimension of
\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
by
q_{A_{r}}=\sum_{i\in A_{r}}\frac{q_{i}}{e(k_{r},i)}
and letting
a
be the constant in (
7.1
), then
\big|2^{m\mathbf{e}_{r}}\mathbf{R}\big|\geq c2^{mq_{A_{r}}}|\mathbf{R}|
.

(2)

If
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}
, its dyadic enlargements need not be in
\mathbb{D}_{S}
because the second kind of restrictions in (
3.13
) are not preserved in general.

7.4.2.
Enlargements by rescaling

We can also enlarge a cube
\mathbf{Q}^{\ell_{i}}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}
be rescaling about its center
\mathbf{c}
.

Definition 7.5
.

If
\mathbf{Q}^{\ell_{i}}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}=\big\{(x_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}:2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}p_{h}\leq x_{h}<2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}(p_{h}+1)\big\}\in\mathbb{D}
and
\rho>0
write

2^{\rho}\circ\mathbf{Q}^{\ell_{i}}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}=\Big\{(x_{h})_{h\in E_{i}}:c_{i}-2^{\rho}2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}\leq x_{h}<c_{i}+2^{\rho}2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}\Big\}
.

The following properties are satisfied.

Lemma 7.6
.

(a)

Given
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
as above, the dyadic enlargement
2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
and the enlargement
2^{m}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
by the dilations (
1.1
) have comparable measures and there is
\delta>0
, depending only on the exponents
\lambda_{h}
and the constant
a
in (
7.1
), such that
2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}\subset 2^{m+\delta}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
.

(b)

If
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}\subset\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{i}}^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, then
2^{m}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}\subset 2^{m}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{i}}^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}
.

Proof.

(a) The side of
2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
in any direction
x_{h}
with
h\in E_{i}
has length
2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}}
and includes the interval
\big[2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}p_{h},2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}(p_{h}+1)\big]
. Therefore it must be contained in the larger interval

\big[2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}(p_{h}+1)-2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}},2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}p_{h}+2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}}\big].

Defining
Q_{i}^{*}=\prod_{h\in E_{i}}\big[2^{-\mu_{\ell,h}}(p_{h}+1)-2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}},2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}p_{h}+2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}}\big]
,
we can notice that it has the same center
\mathbf{c}_{i}
as
\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
. Since
|\mu_{\ell_{i},h}-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}-m\lambda_{h}|\leq 2a
, the scaling factor in the variable
x_{h}
can be estimated by

\Big(\frac{2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}}+2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}+1}}{2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}+1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\lambda_{h}}}=\Big(\frac{1}{2}+2^{\mu_{\ell_{i},h}-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\lambda_{h}}}\leq 2^{m+(2a+1)\gamma_{0}}.

Setting
\gamma_{0}=\max_{1\leq h\leq d}\lambda_{h}^{-1}
,
we then have
2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}\subset Q_{i}^{*}\subset 2^{m+\delta}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
with
\delta=(2a+1)\gamma_{0}
. With
q_{i}
denoting the homogeneous dimension of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
, this gives an inequality
|2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}|\leq 2^{\delta q_{i}}|2^{m}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}|
. Moreover

\displaystyle|2^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}|

\displaystyle=\prod_{h\in E_{i}}2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i}-m,h}+1}\geq\prod_{h\in E_{i}}2^{-\mu_{\ell_{i},h}+m\lambda_{h}-2a}

(7.8)

\displaystyle\geq c_{a}2^{mq_{i}}|\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}|=c_{a}|2^{m}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{i,\mathbf{p}_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}|.

(b) For every
h\in E_{i}
we have the inequalities
\mu(\ell^{\prime}_{i},h)<\mu(\ell_{i},h)
and

2^{-\mu(\ell^{\prime}_{i},h)}p^{\prime}_{h}\leq 2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}p_{h}\leq 2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}(p_{h}+1)\leq 2^{-\mu(\ell^{\prime}_{i},h)}(p^{\prime}_{h}+1).

Simple calculations show that these inequalities imply the following ones:

\displaystyle 2^{-\mu(\ell^{\prime}_{i},h)}\Big(p^{\prime}_{h}+\frac{1}{2}-2^{m-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}\Big(p_{h}+\frac{1}{2}-2^{m-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-\mu(\ell_{i},h)}\Big(p_{h}+\frac{1}{2}+2^{m-1}\Big)\leq 2^{-\mu(\ell^{\prime}_{i},h)}\Big(p^{\prime}_{h}+\frac{1}{2}+2^{m-1}\Big),

which give the required inclusion.
∎

By Proposition
7.2
, Lemma
7.6
also applies to the cubes
\mathbf{Q}^{\ell_{k_{r}}}_{A_{r},\mathbf{p}_{r}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
endowed with the dilations
\delta^{k_{r}}
. Passing to a dyadic rectangle
\mathbf{R}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=(\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{s})\in\mathbb{R}^{s}_{+}
we define

2^{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\circ\mathbf{R}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}2^{\rho_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}.

We also use the notation
2^{\mathbf{m}+\tau}\circ\mathbf{R}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}2^{m_{r}+\tau}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}
with
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{s}
and
\tau>0
.

7.5.
Multi-norm square functions of Plancherel-Pólya type

The notion of Plancherel-Pólya type square functions was introduced by Frazier and Jawerth in
[
14
]
. Once we have defined dyadic rectangles in the multi-norm context, we can define Plancherel-Pólya variants of the multi-norm square functions defined in Section
4.4
and prove
L^{1}
-equivalence between the new and the old ones. We limit ourselves to the Plancherel-Pólya variants of the multi-norm square functions
S_{\Psi}
of tensor type which we will use in later sections, leaving the other cases to the interested reader.

For all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all
L\in\mathcal{L}
denote by
\mathbf{R}^{L}_{\mathbf{x}}
the unique dyadic rectangle
\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}
containing
\mathbf{x}
. With
\Psi_{L}
given in (
4.4
) and for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
define the Plancherel-Pólya norm and the Plancherel-Pólya type square function by

\displaystyle\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}\big|_{\rm PP}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sup\nolimits_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{x}}^{L}}\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{y})\big|\ ;

(7.9)

\displaystyle S^{\rm PP}_{\Psi}f(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}\big|_{\rm PP}(\mathbf{x})^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Theorem 7.7
.

For
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the square functions
S_{\Psi}f
and
S^{\rm PP}_{\Psi}f
have comparable
L^{1}
-norms. The same holds true for the square function
S^{\prime}_{\Sigma}
defined in (
4.8
) and its Plancherel-Pólya-type analogue
{S^{\prime}_{\Sigma}}^{\!\!\rm PP}
.

Proof.

By the trivial point-wise inequality
\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})\big|\leq\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}\big|_{\rm PP}(\mathbf{x})
, it suffices to prove
\|S^{\rm PP}_{\Psi}f\|_{1}\leq C\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}
.
We start from the identity
f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}=\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}}(f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})})*(\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})})
. Applying (
5.7
) with
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
replaced by
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}}\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\mathbf{y})\big|\big|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum\nolimits_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}}\omega_{M,L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\big(1+2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}|\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\big)^{M}\big|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=C\sum\nolimits_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}}\omega_{M,L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\big(1+2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}|\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}-\mathbf{y}_{i}|\big)^{M}\big|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\ .

Fix now
\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}
, with
\mathbb{D}^{L}
defined in (
7.4
),
and assume
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}
, so that
|\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{y}_{i}|\leq C2^{-\ell_{i}}
for all
i=1,\dots,n
. Calling
\mathbf{r}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{y}_{i}
, it follows that

\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}}\big(1+2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}|\mathbf{r}_{i}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\big)^{M}\big|\psi_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})}*\widetilde{\psi}_{i,\ell^{\prime}_{i}}^{(-\ell^{\prime}_{i})}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}\leq C2^{-\gamma_{i}|\ell_{i}-\ell^{\prime}_{i}|}2^{q_{i}\bar{\ell}i}\int\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}}\frac{\big(1+2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}|\mathbf{r}_{i}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\big)^{M}}{\big(1+2^{\bar{\ell}_{i}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\big)^{2M}}\,d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}\ .

Since

1+2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}|\mathbf{r}_{i}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\leq 1+2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}}+2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\leq\begin{cases}1+2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\qquad\quad\ =1+2^{\bar{\ell}_{i}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|&\text{ if }\ell^{\prime}_{i}\leq\ell_{i}\\
2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}}\big(1+2^{\ell_{i}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\big)=2^{\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}}\big(1+2^{\bar{\ell}_{i}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}|\big)&\text{ if }\ell^{\prime}_{i}>\ell_{i}\ ,\end{cases}

we can continue as before and conclude that
\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})\big|\leq C\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}}2^{-\varepsilon|L-L^{\prime}|}\omega_{M,L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})
. Taking the supremum over
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}
and summing over
M
and
L
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Big(\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\Big(\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}\big|_{\rm PP}(\mathbf{y})\Big)^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\Big(\sum\nolimits_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}}2^{-\varepsilon|L-L^{\prime}|}\omega_{M,L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})\Big)^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\omega_{M,L}(\mathbf{y})^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

by Young’s inequality. The result for
S^{\rm PP}_{\Psi}
follows from the proof of Theorem
5.1
. The proof for
{S^{\prime}_{\Sigma}}^{\!\!\rm PP}
is similar and we omit the details.
∎

8.
The multi-norm Hardy space
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

We define
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be the space of distributions
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
S_{\Xi}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
where
S_{\Xi}
is any of the square functions introduced in Sections
5
,
6
and
7.5
. We set
\|f\|_{h^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}=\|S_{\Xi}f\|_{L^{1}}
. Different choices of square function result in equivalent norms. In what follows, the particular square function may vary from one occurrence to another, and will be specified when necessary.
We recall from Section
7.4
the two notions of dyadic enlargement and enlargement by dilations of a given dyadic rectangle.

8.1.
Continuous inclusion of
h^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

Theorem 8.1
.

\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subseteq L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and there is a constant
C
so that
\|f\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\|f\|_{h^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
.

The proof is a direct consequence of Lemmas
8.2
and
8.3
below.

Lemma 8.2
.

\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

Fix a square function
S_{\Psi}f=\big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}\big|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
as in (
4.4
), with the Fourier transform of
\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}
supported on a small neighborhood of
\pi^{-1}(T_{L})
where
\pi
is the log-map in (
3.2
) and
T_{L}
the tube in (
3.15
). Then
\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}=0
for
|L-L^{\prime}|>\kappa
where
\kappa
is the constant in Lemma
3.9
(iv). This implies that
\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}=\sum_{L^{\prime}\leq L}\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}
has Fourier transform supported on
\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\leq 2^{\ell_{i}+\kappa}\ \forall i\}
and equal to 1 on
\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\leq 2^{\ell_{i}-\kappa}\ \forall i\}
. Let
f\in\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
so
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
S_{\Psi}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We prove that
f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}
is in
L^{2}\cap\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}
, for every
L\in\mathcal{L}
, and
\|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}-f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}\to 0
as
L\to\infty
.
First observe that
f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq\sum_{L^{\prime}\leq L}\int\big|f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}
it follows that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq C|L|^{n}\big(\sum_{L^{\prime}\leq L}\int\big|f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{L}\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
. Next, since
f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}=f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}*\Phi_{2L}^{(-2L)}
we have
\|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}\|_{2}\leq\|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}\|_{1}\|\Phi_{2L}^{(-2L)}\|_{2}\leq C^{\prime}_{L}\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
. Thus
f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Next we prove
\|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}-f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}\to 0
as
L\to\infty
. (This implicitly includes the fact that
f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}\in\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}
.) We have

\displaystyle\|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}-f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(\sum\nolimits_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}}\big|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})-f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,d\mathbf{x}.

We split the index set
\mathcal{L}
into three subsets depending on
L
:
E_{L}^{0}=\{L^{\prime}:\ell^{\prime}_{i}<\ell_{i}-2\kappa\ \forall i\}
,
E_{L}^{\infty}=\{L^{\prime}:\ell^{\prime}_{i}>\ell_{i}+2\kappa\ \forall i\}
, and
E_{L}^{1}=\{L^{\prime}:\exists i\text{ such that }\ell_{1}-2\kappa\leq\ell^{\prime}_{i}\leq\ell_{i}+2\kappa\}
.
Because of the support properties, the terms with
L^{\prime}\in E_{L}^{0}
are equal to zero. Those with
L^{\prime}\in E_{L}^{\infty}
are equal to
\big|f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|^{2}
, and
\lim_{L\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(\sum_{L^{\prime}\in E_{L}^{\infty}}\big|f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,d\mathbf{x}=0
by monotone convergence. It remains to consider the terms with
L^{\prime}\in E_{L}^{1}
. The number of these terms grows polynomially in
L
, so that it suffices to prove that each term is controlled by
2^{-\delta|L|}
for some
\delta>0
.
We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}

\displaystyle*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})-f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x}-{\mathbf{y}})-f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,\big|\Phi_{L}^{(-L)}({\mathbf{y}})\big|\,d{\mathbf{y}}\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x}-2^{-L^{\prime}}{\mathbf{y}})-f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}\Big)\big|\Phi_{L}({\mathbf{y}})\big|\,d{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}\Big)\big|\Phi_{L}({\mathbf{y}})\big|\,d{\mathbf{y}}\ ,

where we have set
\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}(\mathbf{x})=\Psi_{L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}-2^{-L^{\prime}}{\mathbf{y}})-\Psi_{L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})
.
We split the integral in
d{\mathbf{y}}
into two parts.

If
|{\mathbf{y}}|>2^{|L^{\prime}|/2}
we use the inequality

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Psi_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq 2\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}\ ,

to conclude that
\int_{|{\mathbf{y}}|>2^{|L^{\prime}|/2}}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}\big)\big|\Phi_{L}({\mathbf{y}})\big|\,d{\mathbf{y}}
decays faster than
2^{-N|L^{\prime}|}
for every
N
. In order to replace
|L^{\prime}|
with
|L|
, observe that, since
L^{\prime}\in E_{L}^{1}
, there is a component
i
such that
|\ell^{\prime}_{i}-\ell_{i}|\leq\kappa
. Using the basic assumptions, for every component
j
we obtain that
\ell_{j}\leq c\ell^{\prime}_{j}
with
c>0
.
This implies that this part of the integral decays faster than
2^{-N|L|}
for every
N
.
If
|{\mathbf{y}}|\leq 2^{|L^{\prime}|/2}
we use instead the inequalities

\big|\partial^{\alpha}\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}(\mathbf{x})\big|=\big|\partial^{\alpha}\Psi_{L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}-2^{-L^{\prime}}{\mathbf{y}})-\partial^{\alpha}\Psi_{L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\big|\leq C_{\alpha,N}\frac{|2^{-L^{\prime}}{\mathbf{y}}|^{\varepsilon}}{(1+|\mathbf{x}|)^{N}}\leq C_{\alpha,N}\frac{2^{-\varepsilon|L^{\prime}|/2}}{(1+|\mathbf{x}|)^{N}}

for every
N
, with
\varepsilon>0
depending on the exponents of the basic dilations on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Hence the Schwartz norms of
\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}
are bounded by a constant times
2^{-\varepsilon|L^{\prime}|/2}
uniformly in
{\mathbf{y}}
.
Observing that
\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}
inherits the cancellations of
\Psi_{L^{\prime}}
, we can then apply Theorem 4.1 with the family
\{\Theta_{L}\}_{L\in\mathcal{L}}
defined by
\Theta_{L}=\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}
if
L=L^{\prime}
and
\Theta_{L}=0
otherwise. It follows that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|f*\Omega_{L^{\prime},{\mathbf{y}}}^{(-L^{\prime})}(\mathbf{x})\big|\,d\mathbf{x}\leq C2^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}|L^{\prime}|/2}\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}=C2^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}|L^{\prime}|/2}\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
,
uniformly in
{\mathbf{y}}
. As above,
|L^{\prime}|
can be changed to
|L|
, correcting the coefficient
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that if
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then
\|f\|_{1}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
.

Proof.

It is now convenient to choose a square function
S_{\Psi}^{\rm PP}
of Plancherel-Pólya type, as in (
7.9
), and assume that the terms
\Psi_{L},\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}
in the corresponding Calderón reproducing formula
f=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}
are localized in space;
i.e.
supported on a common compact neighborhood
U
of
{\mathbf{0}}
rather than in frequency space, cf. Lemma
4.2
. For
f\in L^{2}\cap\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the series
\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}
is convergent in the
L^{2}
-norm. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we define

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\;:\;S_{\Psi}^{{\rm PP}}f(\mathbf{x})>2^{i}\Big\},

(8.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}\;:\;|\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\cap\Omega^{i}|>\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}|,\;|\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\cap\Omega^{i+1}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}|\Big\}.

Each
\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}
belongs to a unique
\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}*f(\mathbf{x})

(8.2)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x}-{\mathbf{y}})\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f({\mathbf{y}})\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}({\mathbf{y}})\,d{\mathbf{y}}

in
L^{2}
-sense. Then, it suffices to show there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\Big\|\sum\nolimits_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\sum\nolimits_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}(\cdot-{\mathbf{y}})\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f({\mathbf{y}})\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}({\mathbf{y}})\,d{\mathbf{y}}\Big\|_{1}\leq C\,2^{i}\,|\Omega_{i}|.

(8.3)

Indeed, combining (
8.1
), (
8.2
), and (
8.3
) we obtain
\|f\|_{1}\leq C\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{i}|\Omega_{i}|\leq C\|S^{\rm PP}_{\Psi}f\|_{1}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
. Now we prove (
8.3
). Since the functions
\{\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}\}
are supported on
U
, there is a constant
\tau_{0}>0
, independent of the scale
L
and
K
, such that

\displaystyle\text{\rm supp\,}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}(\cdot-{\mathbf{y}})\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f({\mathbf{y}})\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}({\mathbf{y}})\,d{\mathbf{y}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}+2^{-L}U\subseteq 2^{\tau_{0}}\circ\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}.

(8.4)

Therefore, the function
f_{i}:=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}(\cdot-{\mathbf{y}})\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f(y)\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}({\mathbf{y}})\,d{\mathbf{y}}

is supported on
\bigcup_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}2^{\tau_{0}}\circ\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}
. Therefore, it follows from the definition (
8.1
) of
\mathcal{B}_{i}
that
f_{i}
is supported on
\Omega^{i}_{(\tau_{0})}
as well, where
\Omega^{i}_{(\tau_{0})}:=\Big\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\;:\;\mathcal{M}_{s}[\chi_{\Omega_{i}}](\mathbf{x})>\frac{1}{(10\cdot 2^{\tau_{0}})^{d}}\Big\}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{s}
is the strong maximal function in
(
5.10
)
. Hence, by Cauchy–Schwarz inequality together with the strong maximal theorem applied to
\chi_{\Omega_{i}}
,

\|f_{i}\|_{1}\leq|\Omega^{i}_{\tau_{0}}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i}\|_{2}\leq C|\Omega_{i}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{i}\|_{2}\ .

(8.5)

By a duality argument,

\displaystyle\|f_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{h\in L^{2},\;\|h\|_{2}=1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(\mathbf{x})f_{i}(\mathbf{x})\,d\mathbf{x}=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}*h({\mathbf{y}})\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f({\mathbf{y}})\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}({\mathbf{y}})\,d{\mathbf{y}}\ .

Using Cauchy–Schwarz and the
L^{2}
-boundedness of square function
h\longmapsto\Big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}|\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}*h|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
we conclude that
\|f_{i}\|_{2}\leq C\big\|\big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}\max_{{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\in R_{K}^{L}}|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|^{2}\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big\|_{2}
. Note that, by the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{i}
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}
we have
\mathcal{M}_{s}(\chi_{(\Omega^{i}_{(\tau_{0})}\setminus\Omega^{i+1})\cap\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}})(\mathbf{x})>\frac{1}{2}
, which implies that, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}({\mathbf{x}})=\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}({\mathbf{x}})^{2}\leq 4\mathcal{M}_{s}(\chi_{(\Omega^{i}_{(\tau_{0})}\setminus\Omega^{i+1})\cap\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}})(\mathbf{x})^{2}
. Hence, by the Fefferman–Stein vector-valued strong maximal inequality

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}\max_{{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\in R_{K}^{L}}|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|^{2}\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f({\mathbf{y}}^{\prime})|^{2}\mathcal{M}_{s}\chi_{(\Omega^{i}_{(\tau_{0})}\setminus\Omega^{i+1})\cap\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}(\mathbf{x})^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\Omega^{i,\tau}_{D}\setminus\Omega^{i+1}}S_{\Psi}^{{\rm PP}}(\mathbf{x})^{2}\,d\mathbf{x}.

Recall that, by (
8.1
),
S_{\Psi}^{{\rm PP}}f(x)\leq 2^{i+1}
for
x\in\Omega^{i}_{(\tau_{0})}\setminus\Omega^{i+1}
, so that, by (
8.4
),

\big\|\big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}\in\mathbb{D}^{L}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}}|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f|^{2}\chi_{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{L}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C2^{2i}|\Omega^{i,\tau}_{D}|.

(8.6)

Finally, (
8.3
) is a consequence of (
8.5
) and (
8.6
).
∎

8.2.
Locality of
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
f\in h_{\mathbf{E}^{1}}
and let
\eta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be supported in a ball of radius
2^{\tau}
with
\tau>0
. Then the product
\eta f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\|\eta f\|_{\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}}
where
C
depends on
\tau
and on some
\mathcal{C}^{k}
-norm of
\eta
.

Proof.

We may assume that
\eta
is supported in
2^{\tau}\circ B(1)
. We use the square function
S_{\Psi}
in (
4.7
) with each
\Psi_{L}
supported in
B(\rho)
and uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm. Then
S_{\psi}[\eta f]
is supported in
2^{\tau}\circ B(1)+B(\rho)\subseteq B(\mu)
for some
\mu>1
depending only on
\tau
,
\rho
, and the exponents
\{\lambda_{h},\,1\leq h\leq d\}
from (
1.1
). By choosing
\rho
sufficiently small, we can make
\mu
arbitrarily close to
2^{\tau}
. We have

\big((f\eta)*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}\big)(\mathbf{x})=\eta(\mathbf{x})(f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)})(\mathbf{x})+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\big[\eta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})-\eta(\mathbf{x})\big]\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{y})\,d\mathbf{y}\ .

There are
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
such that, for
\mathbf{y}\in\text{\rm supp\,}\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}
and
\mathbf{x}\in B(\mu)
, then
|\eta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})-\eta(\mathbf{x})|\lesssim|\mathbf{y}|^{\delta}\lesssim 2^{-\delta^{\prime}|L|}
, with implicit constants depending only on
\tau
and the
C^{1}
-norm of
\eta
. Then

\big|(f\eta)*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})\big|\leq\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x})\big|+C2^{-\delta^{\prime}|L|}\big(|f|*|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}|\big)(\mathbf{x})\ ,

and

\displaystyle\|S_{\Psi}(f\eta)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}+C\int\Big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\big(2^{-\delta^{\prime}|L|}\big(|f|*|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}|\big)(\mathbf{x})\big)^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}+C\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}2^{-\delta^{\prime}|L|}\int\big(|f|*|\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}|\big)(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}+C^{\prime}\|f\|_{1}\lesssim\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}\ ,

by Theorem
8.1
.
∎

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
\tau>0
and
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be supported on
2^{\tau}\circ B(1)
such that
\sum_{P\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\eta(\mathbf{x}-P)=1
. Calling
\eta_{P}(\mathbf{x})=\eta(\mathbf{x}-P)
, there is
A>1
, depending only on
\tau
and some
C^{k}
-norm of
\eta
, such that
\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}\leq\sum_{P\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\|S_{\Psi}(f\eta_{P})\|_{1}\leq A\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}
for every
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

The first inequality is trivial and the second follows from Proposition
8.4
and the bounded overlapping property of the supports of the
S_{\Psi}(f\eta_{P})
.
∎

8.3.
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}
-boundedness of multi-norm singular integrals

The notions of multi-norm Mihlin-Hörmander multiplier and multi-norm singular kernel have been introduced in Theorem
3.11
.
We prove the
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}
-boundedness theorem for multi-norm singular integral operators.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a multi-nom singular kernel. Then the operator
Tf=f*\mathcal{K}
is bounded on
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\|Tf\|_{h^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}\leq C\,\|f\|_{h^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
where
C
depends on the norm of
\mathcal{K}
in
\mathcal{P}_{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proof.

For
i=1,\dots,n
we fix functions
\varphi_{i,\ell_{i}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}})
as in Section
4.1
, imposing the conditions
\text{\rm supp\,}\widehat{\varphi_{i,\ell_{i}}}\subset\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}:|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\leq 2^{3/4}\big\}
and
\widehat{\varphi_{i,\ell_{i}}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i})=1\text{ for }|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\leq 2^{-3/4}
. We define
\psi_{i\ell_{i}}=\varphi_{i,\ell_{i}}-\varphi_{i,\ell_{i}-1}^{(1)}
and, as in Section
4.2
, we construct, for
L\in\mathcal{L}_{D}
,

\Psi_{L}(\mathbf{x})=\big(\prod_{i\in D_{L}}\psi_{i,\ell_{i}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\big)\big(\prod_{i\notin D_{L}}\varphi_{i,\ell_{i}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\big).

For
L\in\mathcal{L}
we have
\delta_{\mathbf{0}}=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}
and

\displaystyle\text{\rm supp\,}\widehat{\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}}

\displaystyle\subset\Big(\prod_{i\in D_{L}}\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}:2^{-\ell_{i}-3/4}\leq|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\leq 2^{-\ell_{i}+3/4}\big\}\Big)\times\Big(\prod_{i\not\in D_{L}}\big\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}:|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}|\leq 2^{-\ell_{i}+3/4}\big\}\Big).

(8.7)

Let
K
be a multi-norm singular kernel and
m=\widehat{K}
the corresponding multiplier.
For
L\in\mathcal{L}
, we define
m_{L}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=m(2^{L}{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\widehat{\Psi_{L}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})
and
\eta_{L}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(m_{L})
. It is easy to deduce from (
8.7
) that the
m_{L}
verify the conditions in Theorem
3.11
(ii) and the
\eta_{L}
the conditions in Theorem
3.11
(iv). Given
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
Tf=f*K=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}f*\eta_{L}^{(-L)}
. With
\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}=\|S_{\Psi}\|_{1}
, we have

\|Tf\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}=\big\|\big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\big|Tf*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}\big|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big\|_{1}\ ,

where

Tf*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}=\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}}f*\eta_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}.

However, the convolution
\eta_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}
is zero if
L
and
L^{\prime}
are not close enough. To see this, consider
the supports of the Fourier transforms of the two factors and their images under
\tau
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. By (
8.7
),
the two images are contained in the
1/4
-enlargements
\widetilde{T}_{L}
of
T_{L}
and
\widetilde{T}_{L^{\prime}}

T_{L^{\prime}}
respectively, i.e.,

\widetilde{T}_{L}=\Big\{{\mathbf{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}:\ell_{j}-\frac{3}{4}\leq t_{j}\leq\ell_{j}+\frac{3}{4}\text{ if }j\in D_{L}\text{ and }0\leq t_{j}\leq\ell_{j}+\frac{3}{4}\text{ if }j\notin D_{L}\Big\}\ ,

and similarly for
T_{L^{\prime}}
. The intersection
\widetilde{T}_{L}\cap\widetilde{T}_{L^{\prime}}
is nonempty if and only if
T_{L}
and
T_{L^{\prime}}
have an edge in common. We can then apply Lemma
3.9
(iv) to conclude that
|L-L^{\prime}|\leq\kappa
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|Tf\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{L}\big|Tf*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{1}=\Big\|\Big(\sum_{L}\big|\sum_{|L^{\prime}-L|\leq\kappa}f*\eta_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|\widetilde{L}|\leq\kappa}\Big\|\Big(\sum_{L}\big|f*\eta_{L+\widetilde{L}}^{(-L-\widetilde{L})}*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{1}\ .

For every
\widetilde{L}
in the above formula, the functions
\Theta_{L}=\eta_{L+\widetilde{L}}^{(-\widetilde{L})}*\Psi_{L}
are uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm and satisfy the other conditions in Proposition
5.8
. This gives the inequality
\|Tf\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
.
∎

8.4.
Characterization of
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by Riesz transforms

By
[
7
]
, for any family
\{\delta_{r}\}_{r>0}
of dilations on a space
\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
, it is possible to construct a system of
\nu
smooth, homogeneous Calderón-Zygmund kernels
\mathcal{K}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{K}_{\nu}
with the property that a function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
belongs to the non-isotropic Hardy space
H^{1}_{\delta}
if and only if
f*\mathcal{K}_{j}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
for all
j=1,\dots,\nu
. One construction of the
\mathcal{K}_{j}
goes as follows. In
\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
with scalar coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu})
consider the Euclidean sphere
S^{\nu-1}=\big\{\mathbf{x}:\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}x_{j}^{2}=1\big\}
and define the
\delta
-homogeneous norm
|\cdot|
by the condition

|\mathbf{x}|=r\ \Longleftrightarrow\delta_{r^{-1}}\mathbf{x}\in S^{\nu-1}.

It follows from the implicit function theorem that this norm is smooth away from the origin.
We define
\mathcal{K}_{j}
by the condition

\widehat{\mathcal{K}_{j}}(\xi)=\frac{\xi_{j}}{|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|^{\lambda_{j}}}\ ,

where
\lambda_{j}
is the exponent of the dilations in the
j
-th entry.

Lemma 8.7
.

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
then
f\in H^{1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
if and only if
f*\mathcal{K}_{j}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
j=1,\dots,\nu
.

Proof.

The sufficient condition (*) in
[
7
, p. 547]
requires that, for any vector
\mathbf{v}=(v_{0},\dots,v_{\nu})\in S^{\nu}
there exist multipliers
\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\nu}
such that
\sum_{j=0}^{\nu}m_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\mu_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=1
and
\sum_{j=0}^{\nu}v_{i}\mu_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=0
. Borrowing formulas from
[
7
, p. 572]
, we define functions
\mu_{j}
, homogeneous of degree zero, by
\mu_{0}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}v_{j}^{2}+v_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}v_{j}\xi_{j}
and
\mu_{j}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=-v_{0}v_{j}-v_{0}^{2}\xi_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq\nu
when
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in S^{\nu-1}
.
∎

Modifying the
\mathcal{K}_{j}
constructed above, we can obtain local Calderón-Zygmund operators characterizing the local Hardy space
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
.

Proposition 8.8
.

For
j=1,\dots,\nu
, let
\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{j}
be the local Calderón-Zygmund kernel such that
\widehat{\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{j}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=\widehat{\mathcal{K}_{j}}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\big(1-\theta({\boldsymbol{\xi}})\big)
, where
\theta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
is supported where
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|\leq 2
and is equal to
1
for
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|\leq 1
. Then a function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
belongs to
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
if and only if
f*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{j}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
for all
j=1,\dots,\nu
.

Proof.

The “only if” part of the proof is standard. We then assume that
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
and
f*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{j}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
for every
j
. Keeping notation and assumptions of Section
4.1
, take
\varphi_{\ell}=\varphi,\psi_{\ell}=\psi=\varphi-\varphi^{(1)}
, with
\widehat{\varphi}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=1
for
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
,
\widehat{\varphi}({\boldsymbol{\xi}})=0
for
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|\geq 1
. Decompose
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
as
f_{0}+f_{1}+f_{\infty}
where
f_{0}=f*\varphi\
,
f_{1}=f*\psi^{(-1)}
, and
f_{\infty}=f-f_{0}-f_{1}=\sum_{\ell\geq 2}f*\psi^{(-\ell)}
. Then
f_{0}*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{j}=0
,
f_{1}*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{j}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
and
f_{\infty}*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{j}=f_{\infty}*\mathcal{K}_{j}
. Then
f_{\infty}*\mathcal{K}_{j}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
for every
j
and, by Lemma
8.7
,
f_{\infty}\in H^{1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})\subset\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
.
To prove that also
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are in
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
we consider the square function

S_{\rm loc}g(\mathbf{x})=\Big(\big|g*\varphi(\mathbf{x})\big|^{2}+\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\big|g*\psi^{(-\ell)}(\mathbf{x})\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Taking
g=f_{0}
or
g=f_{1}
only two values of
\ell
at most give a non-zero term, so that both
S_{\rm loc}f_{0},S_{\rm loc}f_{1}
are in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
.
∎

After these preliminaries for general dilations, we go back to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the
n
-tuple of dilations
\widehat{\delta}^{i}
in (
2.5
). For each
i=1,\dots,n
we denote by
\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{i,j}
,
j=1,\dots,d
the kernels introduced in Proposition
8.8
relative to the dilations
\widehat{\delta}^{i}
.
We also define
\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{i,0}=\delta_{\mathbf{0}}
, the Dirac delta at the origin.

For
J\in\{0,\dots,d\}^{n}
, the
multinorm Riesz kernels

R_{J}=\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{1,j_{1}}*\cdots*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{n,j_{n}}\ .

Notice that, since one or more factors can be
\delta_{\mathbf{0}}
, this family of kernels also contains convolutions of a smaller number of factors, including the single
\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{i,j}
and even
\delta_{\mathbf{0}}
.
It follows from Theorem
3.12
that the distributions
R_{J}
are in
\mathcal{P}_{0}({\mathbf{E}})
. By Theorem
8.6
, each
R_{J}
maps
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into itself, hence into
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We prove the converse statement using a randomization argument as in
[
9
]
.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
f*R_{J}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
J\in\{0,\dots,d\}^{n}
then
f\in\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\|S_{\Psi}f\|_{1}\sim\sum_{J}\|f*R_{J}\|_{1}
.

Proof.

Define the
\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}
-norm using the square function (
6.2
),

S^{\prime\prime}f=\Big(\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\big|f*\big(\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\big)\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

where the
\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}
are as in (
4.6
). Let
f
be as in the hypothesis.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
r_{k}(s)
the
k
-th Rademacher function, defined for
s\in[0,1]
. For any
{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
, we consider the kernel

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{L\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}r_{\ell_{i}}(s_{i})\Big)\Big(\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\Big)=\mathop{*}_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\sum_{\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{N}}r_{\ell_{i}}(s_{i})\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}^{(-\ell_{i})_{i}}\Big)=\mathcal{H}_{s_{1}}*\cdots*\mathcal{H}_{s_{n}},

where each
\mathcal{H}_{s_{i}}
is a local Calderón-Zygmund kernel adapted to the dilations
\delta^{i}
. By Theorem
8.1
and the uniform boundedness of the functions
\sigma_{i,\ell_{i}}
in every Schwartz norm, we have uniform bounds

\|g*\mathcal{H}_{s_{i}}\|_{1}\leq\|g*\mathcal{H}_{s_{i}}\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\delta^{i}}}\leq C\|g\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\delta^{i}}}\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|g*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{i,j}\|_{1}
.
Then

\displaystyle\|f*H_{\mathbf{s}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\big\|(f*\mathcal{H}_{s_{1}}*\cdots*\mathcal{H}_{s_{n-1}})*\mathcal{H}_{s_{n}}\big\|_{1}=\big\|(f*\mathcal{H}_{s_{1}}*\cdots*\mathcal{H}_{s_{n-1}})*\mathcal{H}_{s_{n}}\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big\|(f*\mathcal{H}_{s_{1}}*\cdots*\mathcal{H}_{s_{n-1}})*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{n,j}\|_{1}\ .

In this way we have replaced, in each summand, the term
\mathcal{H}_{s_{n}}
by one of the
\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{n,j}
. We can now continue the chain of inequalities by iteration of the above argument. At the next step, we majorize each term
\big\|(f*\mathcal{H}_{s_{1}}\cdots*\mathcal{H}_{s_{n-1}})*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{n,j}\|_{1}
with
\sum_{j^{\prime}\geq 0}\big\|(f*\mathcal{H}_{s_{1}}*\cdots*\mathcal{H}_{s_{n-2}})*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{n-1,j^{\prime}}*\mathcal{K}^{\flat}_{n,j}\|_{1}
.
Ultimately we obtain the inequality

\|f*H_{\mathbf{s}}\|_{1}\leq C\sum_{J\in\{1,\dots,d\}^{n}}\|f*R_{J}\|_{1}\ ,

uniformly in
{\mathbf{s}}
. It remains to apply Khintchin’s inequality to obtain that

\|S^{\prime\prime}f\|_{1}\leq C\sum\nolimits_{J\in\{1,\dots,d\}^{n}}\|f*R_{J}\|_{1}\ .\qquad\qquad\qquad\qed

Corollary 8.10
.

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\|\mathcal{K}*f\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{1}}
for every multi-norm kernel
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
where
C
depends only on the constants
C_{\alpha,N}
from Definition
3.10
, then
f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{1}}
.

9.
Atomic decomposition of functions in
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

In this section we define
multi-norm atoms
and prove that every function
f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be decomposed
f=\sum_{i}\lambda_{1}a_{i}
where the functions
\{a_{i}\}
are
atoms
and
\sum_{i}|\lambda_{i}|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}}
. Atoms are defined in Section
9.1
, and the decomposition is proved in Section
9.2
.

9.1.
Multi-norm atoms

Multi-norm atoms are functions
a
whose support involves non-isotropic dyadic rectangles and whose size is controlled by certain
L^{2}
estimates. Since
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a local Hardy space, we require that atoms be supported in a ball of (essentially) unit radius. Each atom
a
is associated to a parameter
\tau>0
and to a marked partition
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}\cup\{\emptyset\}
. For
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
this determines the admissible scales of
a
in each variable
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and the required cancellation properties. For
S=\emptyset
atoms have unit size and no required cancellation.

Let
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
. Recall from (
7.4
) that
\mathbb{D}_{S}
is the set of dyadic rectangles
\mathbf{R}^{L}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}\mathbf{Q}_{A_{r}}^{\ell_{k_{r}}}
with multi-scale
L=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n})\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
, and if
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
then
\mathbb{D}_{L}
is the set of dyadic rectangles with multi-scale
L
. If
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded set let
\mathbb{D}_{S}(\Omega)=\big\{\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}:\mathbf{R}\subseteq\Omega\big\}
.

Definition 9.1
.

A
multi-norm
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}
-atom
is a function
a:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
associated with a pair
(S,\tau)
where
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}
and
\tau>0
. There are three kinds, depending on the number
s\geq 0
of marked elements in
S
.

(1)

If
s=0
then
S=\emptyset
and an
(\emptyset,\tau)
-atom
is a function
a
with
\|a\|_{L^{2}}\leq 1
supported on
2^{\tau}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{P}
for some unit cube
\mathbf{\mathbf{Q}}_{P}=\big\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:p_{j}\leq x_{j}<p_{j}+1,\,1\leq j\leq d\big\}
where
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. No cancellation property of
a
is required.

(2)

If
s=1
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
is the only dotted entry for
S
then an
(S,\tau)
-atom
is a function
a
supported on
2^{\tau}\circ\mathbf{R}
for some
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}
with
\|a\|_{L^{2}}\leq|\mathbf{R}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and cancellation in
\mathbf{x}_{k}
;
i.e.

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}}}a(\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k},\ldots)\,d\mathbf{x}_{k}=0
, identically in the variables
\mathbf{x}_{i}
with
i\neq k
.

(3)

If
s\geq 2
and
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
then an
(S,\tau)
-atom

is a function
a
, an associated open set
\Omega
contained in some rescaled unit cube
2^{\tau\circ\mathbf{Q}_{P}}
, and a decomposition
a=\sum_{\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}(\Omega)}a_{\mathbf{R}}
into
pre-atoms
satisfying:

(a)

a_{{\mathbf{R}}}
is supported in
2^{\tau}\circ\mathbf{R}
;

(b)

a_{{\mathbf{R}}}
has cancellation in each dotted variable
\mathbf{x}_{k_{r}}
,
i.e.

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{k_{r}}}}a_{\mathbf{R}}(\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k_{r}},\ldots)\,d\mathbf{x}_{k_{r}}=0
, identically in the other variables;

(c)

if
\mathbb{D}_{S}(\Omega)=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{D}_{j}
is a partition and if
a_{\mathcal{D}_{j}}=\sum_{\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{D}_{j}}a_{\mathbf{R}}
then
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\|a_{\mathcal{D}_{j}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq|\Omega|^{-1}
; in particular,
\|a\|_{L^{2}}\leq|\Omega|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Remarks 9.2
.

1.

The
(\emptyset,\tau)
-atoms having no cancellation appear in the atomic decomposition in
any
local Hardy spaces. See for example
[
18
]
.

2.

When
s=1
and
\mathbf{x}_{k}
is the only dotted variable, the dyadic rectangles
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}
are dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
relative to the dilations
\delta^{k}
in (
3.1
), and the atoms defined in (
2
) are
special
Coifman-Weiss atoms for the associated local non-isotropic Hardy space. The adjective “special” is due to the cancellation in the single variable
\mathbf{x}_{k}
assumed here, which is stronger than cancellation in all variables together, which required in the standard Coifman-Weiss theory. See for example
[
8
]
.

3.

If
s=n
, the atoms defined in (
3
) are
standard
Chang-Fefferman atoms for the product Hardy space relative to the decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
with dilations (
1.1
), with the restriction that only pre-atoms at scale
2^{-L}
with
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S_{p}}
are admitted. See for example
[
3
]
and
[
4
]
.

4.

If
S=\big\{(A_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(A_{s},k_{s})\big\}
with
2\leq s\leq d-1
, the atoms defined in (
3
) are
special
Chang-Fefferman atoms for the product Hardy space relative to the coarser decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{d}=\prod_{r=1}^{s}\mathbb{R}^{A_{r}}
with
\mathbb{R}^{d}
endowed of the dilations
\delta^{S}
defined in (
7.5
). In addition to the restriction in the admissible scales, cancellation of pre-atoms is requested in each dotted variable
\mathbf{x}_{k_{r}}
as opposed to cancellation in each full block of variables
\mathbf{x}_{A_{r}}
, as requested to product atoms.

5.

Notice that for
2\leq s\leq n-1
(which forces
n\geq 3
) the definition of
(S,\tau)
-atom actually depends on the marked partition
S
and not simply on the set
D
of dotted entries.

6.

The proof of Theorem
9.3
provides an atomic decomposition of a function
f\in\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
where the atoms of type (
3
) are sums of pre-atoms depending only on
maximal
rectangles contained in
\Omega
. This is only apparently a stronger property than that required in Definition
9.1
, because, given an atom
a
as in Definition
9.1
(
3
), it is always possible to associate to each pre-atom
a_{\mathbf{R}}
a same maximal rectangle
\mathbf{R}^{\sharp}
containing it and regard the sum of the pre-atoms associated to the same
\mathbf{R}^{\sharp}
as a unique atom
a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R}^{\sharp}}
, so that
a=\sum a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{R}^{\sharp}}
. The possibility of allowing only maximal rectangles may be useful in certain proofs where non-comparability of the different rectangles produces a simplification.

9.2.
Atomic decomposition in
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

In this section we prove that every
f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
admits an
atomic decomposition
. The precise statement is given in the following theorem.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
\tau>0
. There exists
C>0
so that if
f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the for all
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}
, there exists a sequence
\big\{a_{j,S}:j\in\mathbb{Z}\big\}
of
(S,\tau)
-atoms, and a sequence
\big\{\lambda_{j,S}\in\mathbb{C}:j\in\mathbb{Z}\big\}
so that

(a)

f=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{j,S}\,a_{j,S}
with convergence in the distributional sense;

(b)

\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}|\lambda_{j,S}|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
.

The proof makes use of reproducing formulas from Section
4
, constructed via tensor products from functions
\big\{\varphi_{i,\ell_{i}}:\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\big\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}})
that are uniformly bounded in every Schwartz norm and have integral
1
. We shall also suppose that
\varphi_{i,\ell_{i}}
is supported in
\epsilon\circ B_{i}(1)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
where
\epsilon>0
depends on
\tau
is chosen so that condition (
B
) of the following are satisfied.

(A)

If
\Psi_{L},\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}
are as in equation (
4.4
), then
by Lemma
4.2
(
g
) the supports of
\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}
are also contained supported in an
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
;

(B)

If
\Psi_{L},\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}
are as in equation (
4.4
) and if
f
is supported in
2^{\varepsilon}B(1)
then
\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f
is supported in
2^{\tau}\circ B(1)
for every
L\in\mathcal{L}
. If
f
is supported in a dyadic rectangle
\mathbf{R}
then
\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f
is supported in
2^{\tau}\circ\mathbf{R}
.

The existence of such an
\varepsilon
only depends on continuity of homogeneous norms.

Using the partition
\big\{\mathcal{L}_{S}:S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}\big\}
of
\mathcal{L}
, decompose
f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

f=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}}f_{S}\qquad\text{where}\quad\begin{cases}f_{\emptyset}=\Psi_{0}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{0}*f,&\\
f_{S}=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}*f&\text{ for }S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}\ .\end{cases}

(9.1)

We then show that for each
S
there is a decomposition
f_{S}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{j,S}\,a_{j,S}
with
(S,\tau)
-atoms
a_{j,S}
and
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}|\lambda_{j,S}|\leq C\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
. Since there are finitely many marked partitions, this gives the inequality in part (
b
) of the Theorem. With the only excetion of the case
S=\emptyset
, the proof of Theorem
9.3
makes use of the characterization of
\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
using the Plancherel-Pólya type square function
S_{\Psi}^{{\rm PP}}f(\mathbf{x})=\big(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\big|f*\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}\big|_{{\rm PP}}(\mathbf{x})^{2}\big)^{1/2}
defined in (
7.9
), with
\Psi_{L}
satisfying (
A
) and (
B
).

Proof of Theorem
9.3
.

Let
f\in\mathbf{h}_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By Corollary
8.5
we may assume that
f
is supported in
2^{\epsilon}\circ B(1)
with
\varepsilon>0
satisfying (
B
) above, so that every
f_{S}
in (
9.1
) and
S_{\Psi}^{{\rm{\rm PP}}}f
are supported in
2^{\tau}\circ B(1)
. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
define
\Omega^{i}=\big\{\mathbf{x}:S_{\Psi}^{{\rm PP}}f(\mathbf{x})>2^{i}\big\}\subseteq 2^{\tau}\circ B(1)
and
\Omega^{i}_{s}=\big\{\mathbf{x}:\mathcal{M}_{s}[{\mathbf{1}}_{\Omega^{i}}](\mathbf{x})>1/2\big\}
,
\mathcal{M}_{s}
being the local maximal operator in (
5.10
).
The differences
\Omega^{i}\setminus\Omega^{i+1}
partition
\text{\rm supp\,}(S_{\Psi}^{{\rm PP}}f)
and

\|S_{\Psi}^{{\rm PP}}f\|_{L^{1}}\sim\sum\nolimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{i}\,|\Omega^{i}\setminus\Omega^{i+1}|\sim\sum\nolimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{i}\,|\Omega^{i}|\sim\sum\nolimits_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{i}\,|\Omega^{i}_{s}|,

(9.2)

the last equivalence following by
L^{2}
-boundedness of the maximal operator.
For
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}^{*}
, we let
s=0
if
S=\emptyset
and
s
equal to the number of dotted entries if
S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{E}}
.

Case 1:
s=0
. In this case
\mathcal{L}_{\emptyset}=\big\{0\big\}
,
S=\emptyset
, and
\mathbb{D}_{\emptyset}=\big\{\mathbf{R}_{P}^{0}:P\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\big\}
is the set of dyadic unit cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We have
f_{\emptyset}(\mathbf{x})=\Psi_{0}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{0}*f
. Write
\Psi_{0}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{0}*f=\lambda_{0}\,a_{0}
where

\lambda_{0}=\|f\|_{1}
and
a_{0}=\|f\|_{1}^{-1}\Psi_{0}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{0}*f
.
Then
a_{0}
is a multiple (depending only on
\tau
) of a
(\emptyset,\tau)
-atom. In fact,
\text{\rm supp\,}a_{0}\subset 2^{\tau}\circ B(1)
by (
B
), and
\|a_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\widetilde{\Psi}_{0}*\Psi_{0}\|_{2}\leq C
. Moreover

0\leq\lambda_{0}\leq\|f\|_{\mathbf{h}^{1}_{\mathbf{E}}}
.
This completes the proof in case
s=0
.

Case 2:
s=1
.
Adapting the notation in (
8.1
), we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}:\text{$|\mathbf{R}\cap\Omega^{i}|>\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{R}|$ and $|\mathbf{R}\cap\Omega^{i+1}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{R}|$}\Big\},

(9.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\sharp}

\displaystyle=\big\{\mathbf{R}:\text{$\mathbf{R}$ is maximal in $\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i}$ under inclusion}\big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\flat}(\mathbf{R})

\displaystyle=\big\{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i}:\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{R}\big\}\qquad\text{ for }\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\sharp}.

If
\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{B}^{i}_{S}
, then
\mathbf{R}\subseteq\Omega^{i}_{s}
. Moreover, each
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}
belongs to a unique
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i}
, so that the
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i}
form a partition of
\mathbb{D}_{S}
. Moreover, for
s=1
with
k
denoting the unique dotted entry, the rectangles
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{S}
are dyadic cubes of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
relative to the dilations
\delta^{k}
. Because of the intersection properties of dyadic cubes, the elements of
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\sharp}
are pairwise disjoint sets. Hence the
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\flat}(\mathbf{R})
are disjoint subsets of of
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\sharp}
and form a partition of
\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i}
. Denoting by
L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}
the element of
\mathcal{L}_{S}
such that
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}^{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}
, we obtain the following identity:

\displaystyle f_{S}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}*f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{D}_{L}}\int_{\mathbf{R}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}^{(-L)}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})(\Psi_{L}^{(-L)}*f)(\mathbf{y})\,d\mathbf{y}

(9.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\sharp}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\flat}(\mathbf{R})}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\Psi_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}*f(\mathbf{y})\,d\mathbf{y}.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i}
. Then
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\leq 2\mathcal{M}_{s}[{\mathbf{1}}_{\Omega_{s}^{i}\setminus\Omega^{i+1}}](\mathbf{x})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{R}\subseteq\Omega_{s}^{i}
we have

\mathbf{R}=(\mathbf{R}\cap\Omega^{i+1})\cup\big(\mathbf{R}\cap(\Omega_{s}^{i}\setminus\Omega^{i+1})\big)
. Then

|\mathbf{R}|=\big|\mathbf{R}\cap\Omega^{i+1}\big|+\big|\mathbf{R}\cap(\Omega_{s}^{i}\setminus\Omega^{i+1})\big|<\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{R}|+\big|\mathbf{R}\cap(\Omega_{s}^{i}\setminus\Omega^{i+1})\big|

and so
\big|\mathbf{R}\cap(\Omega_{s}^{i}\setminus\Omega^{i+1})\big|>\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{R}|
. This means that if
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}
then
\mathcal{M}_{s}[{\mathbf{1}}_{\Omega_{s}^{i}\setminus\Omega^{i+1}}](\mathbf{x})>\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\sharp}
put

\displaystyle\lambda_{i,\mathbf{R}}

\displaystyle=\big|\mathbf{R}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big|\Big|\Big(\sum\nolimits_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\flat}(\mathbf{R})}\big|\Psi_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}*f\big|^{2}\,{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big|\Big|_{2},

(9.5)

\displaystyle a_{i,\mathbf{R}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i,\mathbf{R}}^{-1}\sum\nolimits_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\flat}(\mathbf{R})}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}*\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\big(\Psi_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}*f\big)\right](\mathbf{x}).

We show that
a_{i,\mathbf{R}}
is a multiple of a
(S,\tau)
-atom, where the multiple depends only on
\tau
and on the
L^{2}
bound of the square function operator
h\longmapsto\big(\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{S}}|\widetilde{\Psi}_{L^{\prime}}^{(-L^{\prime})}*h|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. It follows from properties of
\epsilon
and
\tau
in (
A
) and (
B
) on page
9.3
that
a_{i,\mathbf{R}}
is supported in
2^{\tau}\mathbf{R}
, since the rectangles
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
are contained in
\mathbf{R}
. The cancellation condition follows since
\widetilde{\Psi}_{L}
has cancellation in the variable
\mathbf{x}_{k}
if
L\in\mathcal{L}_{S}
. To show that
\|a_{i,\mathbf{R}}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C|\mathbf{R}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
we use duality and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality as follows. Let
\big[\widetilde{\Psi}_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}\big]^{\vee}(\mathbf{y})=\widetilde{\Psi}_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}(-\mathbf{y})
. Then

\displaystyle\lambda_{i,\mathbf{R}}\|a_{i,\mathbf{R}}

\displaystyle\|_{L^{2}}=\sup_{\|h\|_{2}\leq 1}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(\mathbf{x})\Big(\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{S}^{i,\flat}(\mathbf{R})}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\Psi_{L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}}^{(-L_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})}*f(\mathbf{y})\,d\mathbf{y}\Big)\,d\mathbf{x}\Big|

(9.6)
